Table 1
Leadership as Intentional Influence and Behavior Change
1. Q: What does it mean to say that leadership is intentional influence?
A: Show answer
Chapter 1 presents leadership as the deliberate process of influencing people to change what they do so that a valued result can be achieved. A leader is not defined only by a title, a compelling vision, technical expertise, or the ability to design a plan. Leadership becomes visible when other people adopt and sustain the actions needed to make the intended result real. The word intentional matters: effective leaders do not leave behavior change to chance, hope that people will interpret a message correctly, or assume that enthusiasm will automatically produce action. They study the behaviors that matter, consider why people act as they do, and work systematically to help people act differently. Influence is therefore a practical leadership capability, not merely a communication style or an abstract personal quality.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the chapter's definition in your own words, then identify a real situation in which a leader's success depends on changing other people's actions rather than producing a plan alone."
Key point:
- Leadership is intentional action to influence behavior toward valued results.
- A vision or instruction is not sufficient unless people act differently.
2. Q: Why does the chapter treat behavior change as the defining test of leadership?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that leadership is a human process of cooperative achievement. A leader may articulate an inspiring direction, identify an opportunity, design a new product, or prepare an ambitious plan, but those activities do not by themselves mobilize people to deliver the result. The practical test is whether the people whose actions matter begin behaving differently in a way that advances the objective. This does not mean that every desired outcome is fully under a leader's control; external conditions and resources also matter. It means that when the obstacle depends on human action, leadership must address the behavior rather than stopping at intention, explanation, or authority. If the target behavior does not change, the leadership effort has not yet achieved its central purpose.
Study instruction:
- "Take one organizational objective and list the specific actions that people would need to start, stop, or perform more consistently for the objective to be achieved."
Key point:
- Behavior change is the bridge between leadership intent and execution.
- Measure what people do as well as the result they produce.
3. Q: How is leadership different from creating a vision, challenging the status quo, or designing a business plan?
A: Show answer
Those activities can be important parts of leadership, but Chapter 1 distinguishes them from the work of mobilizing others. A person working alone can imagine a future, propose a new product, challenge an existing practice, or design a detailed growth plan. Leadership adds the human process required to turn those ideas into coordinated action. It involves helping people understand, adopt, and sustain the behaviors needed to execute the plan. A strategy may be intellectually sound yet fail because employees do not report problems, teams do not collaborate, or managers do not follow through. The chapter therefore shifts attention from the quality of the idea alone to the leader's repeatable capacity to influence the people whose actions determine whether the idea succeeds.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a project with a strong written plan but weak execution against one in which people consistently perform the behaviors needed to deliver the plan. Identify the leadership work missing from the first case."
Key point:
- Vision and strategy describe direction; influence mobilizes people to act.
- Execution depends on cooperative human behavior.
4. Q: What distinguishes influence from persuasion tricks or manipulation?
A: Show answer
The authors explicitly distinguish their subject from using verbal tricks, stealth, or a clever combination of persuasive tactics to get people to comply with a leader's wishes. Their concern is deeper and more demanding: changing entrenched human behavior so that people can achieve meaningful results. Manipulation may produce a short-term response without building understanding, capability, commitment, or durable habits. Chapter 1 instead emphasizes studying why people do what they do and deploying a systematic approach to help them act differently. This distinction is important because a temporary agreement, a public promise, or a moment of enthusiasm is not evidence that a difficult behavior has changed. Influence should be evaluated by the quality and durability of the behavior and the value of the result, not by the leader's ability to win an argument.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent change request and distinguish the methods used to secure immediate agreement from the actions intended to support sustained behavior change."
Key point:
- Influence is more than winning agreement or applying persuasive tricks.
- Durable behavior change is the intended outcome.
5. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish meaningful influence from online popularity?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that the contemporary label influencer is often associated with online attention and popularity. Attention can help a message reach people, but it does not establish that the person can change deeply embedded behavior or produce meaningful improvement. A large audience, many followers, or frequent engagement is not the same as a demonstrated capacity to help people adopt difficult behaviors consistently. The authors use this distinction to clarify the book's focus: influence is a leadership capability measured by changes in human action and valued results, not by visibility alone. Popularity may be a communication asset, but it cannot substitute for understanding behavior, designing a coherent change effort, and demonstrating that the change lasts.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a public figure or organizational spokesperson and assess separately their reach, their ability to influence behavior, and the evidence of sustained results."
Key point:
- Reach and popularity are not proof of effective influence.
- Judge influence by behavior and outcomes, not attention alone.
6. Q: Why does the chapter say that influence is relevant to almost every important human challenge?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central proposition is that when a challenge involves people, their behavior is likely to be part of the solution. A company may need safer work practices, a hospital may need more reliable patient-safety behavior, a school may need higher graduation rates, or a family may need to change a damaging habit. These problems differ in subject matter, but each requires people to act differently and repeatedly. The authors are not claiming that technical knowledge, resources, policy, or infrastructure are irrelevant. Rather, those elements often fail to produce their intended value unless people use them in the required way. Influence is therefore a common capability that connects otherwise different personal, organizational, and community problems.
Study instruction:
- "Select a challenge from your own work or community. Identify the people whose actions affect it and explain why a purely technical solution may be insufficient."
Key point:
- Human behavior is a common dependency across many kinds of change.
- Influence is relevant wherever people must act differently.
7. Q: What is the central leadership lesson from Danny Meyer's restaurant example?
A: Show answer
Danny Meyer attributes the distinctive performance of his restaurant group primarily to employee behavior rather than to unique buildings, ingredients, suppliers, or access to a different labor pool. The chapter describes employees who consistently go beyond routine service expectations to create memorable experiences for guests. Meyer's example illustrates that a leader can differentiate an organization by influencing how people behave in ordinary situations, especially when no manager is directing every individual interaction. The lesson is not simply that customer service matters. It is that organizational results can depend on repeated human behaviors that leaders intentionally cultivate across a large workforce.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize Meyer's explanation of his restaurants' distinction and identify at least two employee behaviors that could shape a guest's experience."
Key point:
- Distinctive results can come from distinctive employee behavior.
- Leadership influences the conduct repeated throughout the organization.
8. Q: What does Danny Meyer's responsibility for influencing 1,500 employees to serve 100,000 daily guests reveal about leadership at scale?
A: Show answer
Meyer describes his job as influencing approximately 1,500 people to create special experiences for approximately 100,000 customers every day. The scale matters because he cannot personally deliver every experience or supervise every interaction. His leadership challenge is to shape the behavior of many employees so that the intended standard appears repeatedly across locations and customer situations. This requires more than being personally excellent at hospitality; it requires a way of influencing others to perform consistently. The example shows why leaders must think beyond individual acts and consider how their influence reaches people who are not in direct contact with them.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the approximate number of daily guests per employee using the chapter's illustrative figures, then explain why the leader must influence a workforce rather than serve each guest personally."
Key point:
- Leadership at scale works through the behavior of many people.
- Consistency across individual interactions creates the overall experience.
9. Q: Why did Meyer emphasize behavior rather than restaurants' physical assets or products?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports Meyer's view that competitors used the same labor pool, bought from the same vendors, and produced similarly delicious food. He therefore saw employee behavior as the distinguishing factor behind his group's reputation. This does not establish that facilities, ingredients, recruitment, or product quality never matter. It shows that when competitors have broadly comparable resources, the way employees treat customers can become a major source of difference. A leader who focuses only on assets and technical inputs may overlook the human actions through which those inputs become an actual customer experience. The practical lesson is to examine what employees repeatedly do at the point where the organization's value is delivered.
Study instruction:
- "For a service organization, compare its physical and technical resources with the behaviors customers experience directly. Identify which behaviors could differentiate its service."
Key point:
- Resources enable service, but behavior shapes how service is experienced.
- Examine the human actions that turn organizational inputs into value.
10. Q: What does Carlo's response to the customer who lost her purse demonstrate about influence?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's Gramercy Tavern story, an employee notices a distressed guest, listens to the problem, reassures her, arranges repeated calls to the missing phone, coordinates with the taxi driver, travels to meet the driver, pays for the driver's trouble, and returns the purse. The example illustrates initiative, attentiveness, practical problem-solving, and a willingness to take responsibility for the guest's experience. Its significance is not that every employee should independently incur any expense or make any promise. Rather, the story shows how an employee's behavior can embody the service standard a leader wants customers to experience. It also raises a practical management question: what expectations, discretion, support, and boundaries allow staff to respond constructively when unusual situations arise?
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the sequence of Carlo's actions and identify the observable behaviors that transformed a stressful incident into a positive customer experience."
Key point:
- Influence becomes visible in specific actions during real situations.
- A service principle matters when employees enact it under pressure.
11. Q: How does the chapter connect individual acts of exceptional service with organizational culture?
A: Show answer
The authors emphasize that Carlo's action was remarkable not only because it happened, but because similar actions routinely occurred across Meyer's restaurants. A single heroic incident could be attributed to an unusually generous individual. Repeated conduct across employees and locations suggests that the organization has developed patterns of behavior that go beyond isolated personal initiative. The chapter attributes this consistency to Meyer's systematic and intentional actions aimed at influencing specific behavior. Culture, in this context, is not merely a slogan displayed on a wall; it is reflected in what people repeatedly do when they interact with customers and solve problems. Leaders should therefore look for evidence of routine behavior, not just memorable anecdotes.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an isolated example of excellent service with evidence that the same behavior occurs reliably across a team. Identify what additional evidence would demonstrate a shared pattern."
Key point:
- Repeated behavior is stronger evidence of culture than a single story.
- A leader's influence should become visible in routine practice.
12. Q: What does it mean for influence to be systematic and intentional?
A: Show answer
A systematic and intentional approach means that leaders deliberately identify the behavior they want to change, study the reasons people currently act as they do, and use repeatable actions to support a different pattern. It is different from reacting to each failure with an improvised speech, issuing a general reminder, or hoping that a new policy will be followed. The chapter's examples show leaders who think about behavior, discuss it, and practice ways to influence it. A systematic approach can be examined, repeated, learned, and improved rather than depending entirely on the personality or presence of one exceptional leader. Chapter 1 introduces this discipline but does not yet specify every component of the full influence model developed later in the book.
Study instruction:
- "Take a current improvement initiative and write down its target behavior, the reason it matters, and the deliberate actions leaders will use to influence it. Separate these from general messages or aspirations."
Key point:
- Intentional influence is planned and repeatable, not accidental.
- Leaders study behavior and practice ways to change it.
13. Q: What does the chapter mean by rapid, profound, and sustainable behavior change?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes true leaders as having a repeatable capacity to influence change that is rapid, profound, and sustainable. Rapid change concerns the time needed to begin producing the desired behavior and results; profound change concerns the significance or depth of the change rather than a superficial response; sustainable change concerns whether the new behavior continues instead of disappearing after attention shifts elsewhere. These dimensions should be considered together. A short-lived improvement may be fast but not sustainable, while a small compliance response may occur quickly without changing an entrenched habit. The chapter's standard is therefore more demanding than securing a temporary commitment or achieving a one-time performance spike.
Study instruction:
- "For a behavior-change initiative, define what would count as rapid progress, meaningful depth, and evidence that the new behavior has lasted over time."
Key point:
- Effective influence seeks speed, depth, and durability.
- A temporary response is not the same as sustainable change.
14. Q: Why must leaders connect changed behavior to valued results?
A: Show answer
Behavior change is not an end in itself. The chapter links influence to producing valued results, such as better customer experiences, reliable software delivery, safer work, improved public health, or stronger organizational performance. A leader should therefore be able to explain why a particular behavior matters and how it contributes to the result being pursued. Without that connection, teams may focus on visible activity that does not solve the underlying problem. Conversely, a desired result stated too broadly may not tell people what to do differently. Leaders need to connect the outcome to the actions that make it more likely, then examine whether those actions are producing the intended value.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a desired result and build a simple cause-and-effect explanation linking it to the specific behaviors people must perform. Identify assumptions that need testing."
Key point:
- Behavior change should serve a clearly valued result.
- Activity alone is not evidence of meaningful improvement.
15. Q: How did Rich Sheridan reframe the software performance problem at Menlo Innovations?
A: Show answer
After years of missed deadlines and buggy code in large, complex software projects, Rich Sheridan concluded that he needed to focus less on the code and more on the humans who created it. This reframing changed the unit of analysis. Instead of treating quality and schedule failures only as technical defects, he examined the behavior of the people and teams producing the software. The chapter uses this example to show that a technical performance problem may have important behavioral causes. It does not suggest that code quality or engineering methods are unimportant; it shows that technical solutions alone may not address patterns such as silence about problems or delayed communication about commitments.
Study instruction:
- "Take an example of repeated project failure and separate the technical symptoms from the human behaviors that may be contributing to them. Identify evidence needed before concluding that behavior is a cause."
Key point:
- Look beyond the work product to the behavior of the people producing it.
- Human behavior can be a critical part of a technical performance problem.
16. Q: Which two vital behaviors did Sheridan seek to establish at Menlo Innovations?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies two behaviors: employees should admit when they have problems, and they should speak up immediately when they will not meet a deadline. These are specific and observable actions, not broad instructions to communicate better or work harder. The first behavior makes difficulties visible instead of allowing people to conceal them. The second gives colleagues and managers timely information to adjust plans, coordinate dependencies, or address obstacles. The chapter presents these behaviors as central to more consistent software delivery. Their value lies in the way they improve the flow of information needed to manage quality and schedule risks.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize the two behaviors and rewrite each as an observable action with a clear trigger, such as discovering a problem or recognizing a deadline risk."
Key point:
- Vital behaviors should be concrete enough to recognize in practice.
- Early admission and escalation make problems manageable.
17. Q: Why were admitting problems and reporting deadline risks important to software delivery?
A: Show answer
In a large software project, an undisclosed problem can remain hidden while other work depends on an incorrect assumption. A missed deadline that is reported only after it occurs leaves less time to re-sequence tasks, adjust expectations, obtain help, or protect quality. By contrast, admitting a problem and speaking up as soon as a commitment is at risk provide information while corrective choices may still be available. The chapter reports that when Menlo teams performed these behaviors consistently, products were completed correctly and on time. The underlying lesson is that communication behavior can influence technical quality and delivery reliability because it changes when and how risks become visible.
Study instruction:
- "Map a project's dependencies and identify the point at which a problem or schedule risk must be reported to give others a meaningful opportunity to respond."
Key point:
- Early visibility supports better coordination and correction.
- Timely communication can protect both quality and delivery.
18. Q: Why might employees be reluctant to admit failure or disclose that a deadline is at risk?
A: Show answer
Chapter 1 highlights the strong human reluctance to admit failure, describing it as a behavior many people find exceptionally difficult. Employees may fear embarrassment, criticism, loss of status, disappointing a manager, or being judged as incompetent. They may also hope to solve the problem privately before anyone notices, even when the delay makes the situation worse. These reactions help explain why a simple instruction to speak up may not change behavior. Leaders must understand the pressures and expectations surrounding silence and disclosure rather than assuming that employees lack knowledge of the rule. The chapter does not catalogue every possible cause, so leaders should investigate the actual conditions in their own teams.
Study instruction:
- "Ask employees what makes early disclosure difficult in a specific team. Compare stated expectations with the way managers actually respond when problems are reported."
Key point:
- Knowing the desired behavior does not remove the pressures that discourage it.
- Leaders must understand why silence feels safer than disclosure.
19. Q: Why is a single speech or motivational message unlikely to change an entrenched behavior reliably?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that people face powerful inducements to remain silent or preserve familiar habits. A speech can communicate an expectation, but it does not necessarily change the pressures, routines, assumptions, or practical barriers that shape daily choices. Employees may agree with a message in a meeting and still conceal a problem when they later fear criticism or believe they can fix it alone. Similarly, enthusiasm can fade when people return to established work patterns. The authors therefore reject reliance on pep talks, bribes, or clever communication as sufficient solutions to difficult behavior problems. Leaders need a broader, repeatable strategy that addresses the forces influencing behavior over time.
Study instruction:
- "Review a change initiative that relied mainly on a launch presentation or reminder. Identify the practical barriers and recurring pressures that remained after the message was delivered."
Key point:
- Communication can introduce a change but rarely sustains it by itself.
- Entrenched habits require a systematic influence strategy.
20. Q: How should a leader distinguish a result problem from a behavior problem?
A: Show answer
A result problem describes the gap between current performance and the outcome the organization wants, such as missed deadlines, poor service, unsafe work, or low retention. A behavior problem asks what people are doing, failing to do, or doing inconsistently that contributes to that gap. The two are connected but not interchangeable. A project may be late, but the actionable behaviors could include delaying escalation, failing to disclose defects, or not coordinating dependencies. Leaders should not assume that every result gap is caused only by behavior; inadequate resources, flawed processes, technology, or external conditions may also matter. The chapter's approach is to investigate human behavior as a key part of the problem rather than stopping at the outcome label.
Study instruction:
- "For one performance gap, write the result statement and then identify two or three candidate behaviors that could contribute to it. Mark which causes remain unverified."
Key point:
- Results describe what is happening; behaviors help explain how it happens.
- Investigate behavioral causes without ignoring structural or technical causes.
21. Q: How can a leader identify the specific behaviors that matter most to a change effort?
A: Show answer
Begin with the valued result and work backward to the actions that directly influence it. The Menlo example shows the benefit of identifying a small number of vital behaviors rather than relying on broad goals such as improving teamwork or increasing accountability. A useful behavior statement describes an action that can be observed, such as reporting a problem when it is discovered or notifying colleagues when a deadline becomes unrealistic. Leaders should check whether the behavior is genuinely connected to the desired result and whether people can recognize when it is expected. The chapter illustrates this method through examples but does not provide a complete diagnostic worksheet; further analysis is needed to validate the chosen behaviors in each setting.
Study instruction:
- "Translate a broad goal such as better collaboration into observable actions. Test each proposed behavior by asking when it should occur and how another person would recognize it."
Key point:
- Specific vital behaviors make change actionable.
- Select behaviors because they influence the result, not because they sound desirable.
22. Q: Why should leaders investigate why people behave as they do before trying to change them?
A: Show answer
The chapter repeatedly emphasizes understanding why people do what they do and what it takes to help them act differently. Without that diagnosis, a leader may prescribe a solution that addresses the wrong cause. For example, an employee who fails to report a problem may lack confidence, fear a negative response, be uncertain about escalation expectations, or believe the issue is not serious enough. Each possibility suggests a different intervention. A generic reminder may not address any of them. Studying the reasons behind behavior helps leaders design influence efforts that fit the real situation and avoid blaming people for actions shaped by the environment around them.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a behavior that needs to change and list several plausible reasons it occurs or fails to occur. Identify what evidence or conversations would help distinguish among those explanations."
Key point:
- Diagnose the reasons behind behavior before selecting an intervention.
- The same visible behavior can have different underlying causes.
23. Q: What does the chapter mean by deploying a critical mass of sources of influence?
A: Show answer
Chapter 1 states that success depends on deploying a critical mass of the sources of influence that shape human behavior. At this introductory stage, the authors signal that behavior is affected by multiple sources rather than by a single message or incentive. The practical implication is that leaders should not assume one lever will overcome every barrier to an entrenched habit. They need to consider the range of forces that encourage, discourage, enable, or obstruct the target behavior and build an influence effort strong enough to address the important ones. The chapter does not yet define the full categories or mechanics of the model, so readers should not treat this introductory statement as a complete list of influence sources.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a single reminder may be insufficient for a difficult habit. Note the different kinds of forces that could affect the behavior, without assigning categories not yet explained in this chapter."
Key point:
- Behavior is shaped by multiple sources of influence.
- A durable change effort must address more than one isolated lever.
24. Q: Why are isolated tactics such as pep talks, bribes, or social-media expertise insufficient for difficult change?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts deep behavior change with approaches that depend on enthusiasm, rewards, or attention. Such tactics may sometimes support a change effort, but the authors argue that they are not enough to alter entrenched habits reliably. A person may respond temporarily to a reward or public message while continuing to face the same pressures and routines that produced the original behavior. The chapter's examples involve repeated actions across teams, organizations, and communities, not merely one-time compliance. The practical lesson is not to ban communication or incentives, but to avoid mistaking a single tactic for a complete influence strategy.
Study instruction:
- "Take a behavior-change campaign and identify which elements rely on a single tactic. Explain what other influences or practical barriers would need to be examined for the behavior to persist."
Key point:
- One tactic rarely overcomes every force shaping an entrenched habit.
- Use a coordinated approach rather than relying on a motivational event.
25. Q: How can leaders make desired behavior repeatable instead of depending on occasional heroics?
A: Show answer
The Meyer example contrasts one extraordinary act with the fact that similar actions routinely occur across the restaurant group. The Menlo example similarly describes two behaviors becoming consistent across teams rather than appearing only when an individual manager intervenes. These examples point to repeatability as a central leadership objective. Leaders need to make the desired action understandable, expected, practicable, and reinforced in the situations where it is needed. They should observe whether the behavior occurs across people and over time, not only whether one employee performs it once. The chapter attributes repeatability to systematic influence but leaves the detailed design of the full system to the wider book.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a behavior currently associated with one high-performing individual. Identify what would need to change for the same behavior to become a reliable team practice."
Key point:
- A strong system produces repeatable behavior beyond individual heroes.
- Look for consistency across people, situations, and time.
26. Q: How did the authors organize their research into effective influence?
A: Show answer
The authors describe three complementary forms of study: the literature, the leaders, and the learners. They first examined published work to identify people who had mastered aspects of influence and achieved changes considered difficult or unlikely. They then located and studied practitioners whose work demonstrated those changes in real settings. Finally, they observed thousands of parents, supervisors, managers, and executives learning and applying the skills. This combination matters because published ideas, field examples, and learner experience provide different perspectives. The authors sought not merely to explain success after the fact, but to identify principles that people could learn and use repeatedly.
Study instruction:
- "Recall the three research streams and explain what each contributes: conceptual knowledge from literature, practical evidence from leaders, and application experience from learners."
Key point:
- The research combines literature, practitioners, and learners.
- A useful leadership method must connect ideas with repeatable practice.
27. Q: What was the purpose of reviewing more than 17,000 articles and books?
A: Show answer
The authors report that their research team reviewed more than 17,000 articles and books to identify scholars and practitioners who had mastered aspects of influence. The purpose was to search broadly for examples of rapid, profound, and sustainable behavior change that many people considered impossible. The figure indicates the scale of the literature review described by the authors; it should not be interpreted as proof that every item was equally relevant, independently evaluated, or included in a formal meta-analysis. The important point for the reader is the breadth of the search and the effort to identify recurring principles across different areas of research and practice.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what a broad literature review can contribute to a leadership model and distinguish the authors' reported search volume from a formal statistical evidence rating."
Key point:
- The authors used a broad literature search to identify effective influence work.
- A large search volume is context, not a substitute for evaluating the quality of evidence.
28. Q: Why did the authors study successful practitioners directly rather than relying only on published explanations?
A: Show answer
The authors wanted to examine how people actually produced difficult changes, not merely read descriptions of success after the outcome was known. They tracked down leaders and closely examined their work in the settings where change occurred. This approach can reveal how principles are applied under real constraints, how leaders respond to resistance, and whether a method is repeatable. It also helps distinguish an inspiring success story from a practical process that others might learn. The chapter presents practitioner cases as evidence that influence can be deliberately developed, while detailed mechanisms for each case are explored elsewhere in the book.
Study instruction:
- "For a leadership case study, identify what information would be needed beyond the final result: the target behavior, context, barriers, actions taken, and evidence that change lasted."
Key point:
- Studying practitioners reveals how influence is used in real settings.
- A result story is more useful when the process behind it can be examined.
29. Q: What does Wiwat Rojanapithayakorn's Thai HIV-prevention work illustrate about influence?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Wiwat as a leader who helped prevent more than five million Thai citizens from contracting HIV/AIDS, despite having little or no organizational power when he began. The example illustrates that influence can operate at a population scale and does not necessarily depend on formal authority over every person whose behavior must change. It also demonstrates the authors' interest in leaders who achieve changes that initially appear improbable. The chapter does not provide the full intervention design in this introductory discussion, so it would be inaccurate to invent the exact techniques or attribute a specific causal mechanism to the case based only on this passage.
Study instruction:
- "Record the reported scale of the Thai HIV-prevention result and explain what the example suggests about influence beyond formal organizational authority. Do not infer methods the chapter has not described."
Key point:
- Influence can reach far beyond a leader's direct authority.
- Large-scale change still depends on human behavior.
30. Q: What additional lesson does the chapter draw from Wiwat's later smoking-cessation assignment?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that, years after the HIV-prevention work, Wiwat was asked by his government to help nine million people stop smoking within five years. At the time the book was written, he was reportedly on track to complete the task a couple of years early. This example extends the argument from one public-health challenge to another and suggests that effective leaders can transfer a disciplined approach to different behaviors. It should be presented as the status reported by the chapter at publication, not as a current forecast or a verified statement about the eventual completion date. The key lesson is the portability of influence principles across distinct public-health objectives.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the smoking assignment supports the idea of transferable influence capability. Preserve the chapter's time-qualified wording when referring to the reported progress."
Key point:
- Influence capability can be applied to different behavior-change challenges.
- Historical progress claims should not be restated as current results.
31. Q: What do the examples of Martha Swai, Dain Hancock, and Antanas Mockus show about the range of influence challenges?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights Martha Swai's work to reduce spousal abuse in Tanzania using a distinctive radio program, Dain Hancock's effort to influence a cynical and resistant 13,000-person workforce at Lockheed Martin Aeronautics, and Antanas Mockus's response to a severe water shortage in Bogotá, where water use reportedly fell by 40 percent within months. The cases differ in social issue, organizational scale, and public context. Together they illustrate the breadth of problems the authors believe can be addressed through influence. The introductory chapter does not explain every intervention detail, so the examples should be used to understand the range of applications rather than to claim a particular technique produced each result.
Study instruction:
- "Create a comparison note for the three cases covering the challenge, population or workforce, reported outcome, and what the chapter does not yet explain about the method."
Key point:
- Influence applies across social, corporate, and community challenges.
- Do not infer case mechanisms that the chapter has not described.
32. Q: How can the same influence principles apply across very different sectors?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects restaurant hospitality, software development, public health, workplace safety, education, law enforcement, poverty reduction, and family behavior through a common requirement: people must adopt actions that support a valued result. The context changes, and the behaviors will not be identical, but the leadership task of understanding human action and influencing sustained change remains relevant. This does not mean that one intervention can be copied unchanged from a restaurant to a hospital or a community campaign. The transferable element is the disciplined way of thinking about behavior; the specific actions and safeguards must fit the setting, people, risks, and objective.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two sectors from the chapter and compare their desired outcomes. Identify the common influence challenge and the context-specific behaviors that would differ."
Key point:
- The influence discipline can transfer across sectors.
- Adapt the target behavior to the context rather than copying a tactic mechanically.
33. Q: What does influence without formal authority mean for leaders and change agents?
A: Show answer
Wiwat's example is presented as a case in which the leader began with little to no organizational power yet influenced behavior across a very large population. This suggests that formal position is not the only route through which change can be mobilized. A person may influence others through credibility, relationships, a compelling understanding of the problem, coordinated action, or other mechanisms that the chapter's broader model will explore. The passage does not imply that authority is never useful or that anyone can change an entire population through personal appeal alone. It demonstrates that leaders should not treat lack of hierarchical control as proof that meaningful influence is impossible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a change objective over which you have limited formal authority. List the people whose cooperation is needed and the relationships or channels through which their behavior could be influenced."
Key point:
- Formal authority is not the only basis for influence.
- A change agent still needs a deliberate strategy for mobilizing others.
34. Q: What evidence does the chapter offer that influence skills can be learned and applied by ordinary leaders?
A: Show answer
The authors report observing thousands of parents, supervisors, managers, and executives learning the skills and applying them in their own settings. They provide examples of changes led by furniture retailers, telecommunications executives, healthcare managers, school leaders, financial professionals, and others. These examples support the claim that influence is not reserved for a small group of naturally charismatic individuals. The authors present it as a set of principles and skills that can be studied, practiced, and used in different roles. The evidence described in Chapter 1 is illustrative rather than a complete evaluation of every learner's results, so it should support the learning proposition without implying that every attempt will succeed.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three learner examples from the chapter and explain what each suggests about the range of people who can apply influence skills."
Key point:
- Influence is presented as a learnable leadership capability.
- Practice and application matter more than relying on charisma alone.
35. Q: How can influence support better results in safety, quality, service, and retention?
A: Show answer
The chapter lists cases involving reduced delivery damage, stronger innovation, better stakeholder participation in decisions, more consistent hospital customer service, improved nursing retention, safer food-pantry access, greater cross-selling, safer mining practices, and longer principal retention. These examples show that influence can be relevant to both operational reliability and human outcomes. The shared logic is that desired performance depends on people carrying out behaviors consistently, not simply on management announcing a target. However, the chapter does not provide the full causal analysis for every case, so leaders should treat the examples as evidence of possible applications and examine the specific behavior and context before adopting an intervention.
Study instruction:
- "Select one listed outcome and define the employee or stakeholder behaviors that could plausibly contribute to it. Identify what local evidence would be needed to validate the connection."
Key point:
- Influence can support both operational and people-related outcomes.
- Translate a desired outcome into context-specific behavior before acting.
36. Q: What warning does the chapter give about the frequency of failed change efforts?
A: Show answer
The authors report that their review of three decades of change literature found fewer than one in eight workplace change efforts produced more than cynicism. They also cite low success rates for attempts to change personal habits such as excessive spending and inadequate exercise. These figures are used to emphasize how difficult behavior change can be and why casual or improvised approaches often disappoint. They should be attributed to the authors' account in the chapter rather than treated as universal current statistics for every organization, population, or intervention. The practical message is to take change design seriously, examine evidence, and avoid assuming that announcing a new initiative will cause people to behave differently.
Study instruction:
- "When using the chapter's failure-rate claims, preserve their attribution and scope. Discuss why a change initiative should be evaluated by actual behavior and outcomes rather than launch activity."
Key point:
- The chapter portrays sustained behavior change as difficult and often unsuccessful.
- Treat the cited rates as the authors' reported review, not universal benchmarks.
37. Q: What common failure patterns does Chapter 1 identify in attempts to change behavior?
A: Show answer
The chapter criticizes approaches that rely on slogans, one-off speeches, pep talks, bribes, verbal tricks, or online attention as if they were sufficient to change entrenched habits. It also describes a general lack of personal theories, shared language, models, and practiced skills for influencing behavior. In the Menlo example, silence about problems and deadline risks persisted despite the obvious importance of delivery performance. These patterns point to a gap between wanting a result and deliberately shaping the actions that produce it. A leader should therefore look for missing diagnosis, vague behavioral expectations, weak follow-through, and dependence on isolated tactics when an initiative fails.
Study instruction:
- "Review a failed change effort and identify whether it relied on a slogan, a single event, an incentive, or a broad aspiration without defining and supporting the required behavior."
Key point:
- Good intentions and launch activity do not guarantee behavior change.
- A weak influence strategy often leaves the underlying habit untouched.
38. Q: How should an organization evaluate whether an influence effort is working?
A: Show answer
The chapter's standard suggests examining both the target behavior and the valued result. Leaders should ask whether people are performing the specified actions, how consistently the actions occur across the relevant group, and whether the intended result is improving. They should also distinguish an early response from a durable pattern, because the authors emphasize rapid, profound, and sustainable change. For example, a team may report problems more often after a new expectation is introduced; that can be a positive behavioral change even before delivery performance improves. Measures should therefore include observable behavior and outcome indicators, interpreted in context rather than treated as interchangeable.
Study instruction:
- "For one initiative, define at least one observable behavior measure, one outcome measure, and one follow-up point for checking whether the change persists."
Key point:
- Evaluate behavior and results together.
- Sustainability requires checking whether the new behavior continues.
39. Q: What are the limits of the evidence and detail presented in Chapter 1?
A: Show answer
Chapter 1 introduces the authors' thesis and uses selected success stories to show that intentional influence can produce meaningful change across many settings. It does not provide the complete influence model, detailed intervention steps, or a full evaluation of every case cited. Some outcomes are reported as examples without all underlying methods, comparison groups, or contextual details in this chapter. Readers should therefore avoid assuming that every case proves the same causal mechanism or that an intervention can be copied without adaptation. The chapter is a foundation for the book's argument; the detailed methods and evidence need to be examined in the later material and in the relevant context before implementation.
Study instruction:
- "For each case you cite, separate what Chapter 1 explicitly reports from what you would still need to investigate, such as intervention design, context, measurement, and alternative explanations."
Key point:
- Chapter 1 establishes the thesis rather than presenting the entire model.
- Distinguish illustrative cases from fully documented causal evidence.
40. Q: How could an NGO finance leader apply the chapter's influence principles to improve early reporting of financial problems?
A: Show answer
Suppose a finance team repeatedly receives notice of budget overruns, unsupported expenditure, or partner reporting delays only after the month-end deadline. A purely procedural response might issue another reminder or circulate the reporting calendar. Applying Chapter 1's approach, the leader would first define the vital behaviors, such as notifying the finance focal point as soon as a cost is forecast to exceed budget, flagging missing partner evidence when identified, and escalating a deadline risk before the due date. The leader would then investigate why staff remain silent: fear of blame, unclear thresholds, uncertainty about whom to contact, or lack of confidence in the response. The resulting influence effort should make early disclosure practicable and routine while preserving formal approval, documentation, and escalation requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Write three observable early-reporting behaviors for a finance team. Identify at least three possible reasons staff might delay disclosure and the evidence needed to test each reason."
Key point:
- Translate the chapter's behavioral focus into specific finance-team actions.
- Early visibility supports control and corrective action; it does not replace formal procedures.
41. Q: How could a program or operations manager use influence to improve cross-functional collaboration?
A: Show answer
A manager facing recurring handover failures should avoid treating collaboration as an abstract attitude. The chapter's approach suggests identifying the actions that would make cooperation observable, such as confirming ownership of a dependency, sharing a risk when it first becomes apparent, and notifying affected colleagues when a commitment changes. The manager should then examine why people do not perform those actions consistently. Competing priorities, unclear responsibilities, past negative reactions, or weak coordination arrangements may contribute. The objective is to establish repeatable behaviors that improve delivery, not simply to ask everyone to be more collaborative. The specific practices should be adapted to the team's workflow and decision rights.
Study instruction:
- "Take one recurring cross-functional failure and define the behavior expected from each team at the handover point. Identify how the manager will observe whether the behavior is becoming routine."
Key point:
- Collaboration improves when expectations are translated into observable actions.
- Diagnose barriers before repeating a general request to cooperate.
42. Q: What high-level influence process can a leader derive from Chapter 1?
A: Show answer
A practical sequence consistent with the chapter is to define the valued result, identify the human behaviors that materially affect it, investigate why those behaviors currently occur or fail to occur, and design deliberate actions to influence the relevant forces. The leader then observes whether the behavior changes, checks whether the result improves, and refines the approach so the change becomes repeatable and sustainable. This is a high-level synthesis of the chapter's argument, not a substitute for the complete model described in the rest of the book. Its value is that it prevents leaders from jumping directly from a performance gap to a motivational message without understanding the behavior that must change.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the sequence to a real challenge and document the result, target behaviors, suspected behavioral causes, influence actions, and evidence you would monitor."
Key point:
- Start with the result, but design the change around behavior.
- Treat influence as a cycle of diagnosis, action, observation, and refinement.
43. Q: How can leaders build an organizational capability for influence rather than run isolated change campaigns?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes effective leaders as people who think about influencing behavior, talk about it, and practice it. This suggests that influence should become a repeatable management discipline rather than an occasional project launched only when performance deteriorates. Leaders can build that capability by making behavioral diagnosis part of planning, discussing specific actions during team reviews, learning from successful and unsuccessful attempts, and developing managers who can apply the principles in different situations. The chapter's examples show this capability operating across many levels and sectors. It does not mean every problem needs a large campaign; it means leaders consistently consider the human behaviors on which important results depend.
Study instruction:
- "Review a management routine such as a monthly performance meeting. Identify how it could include discussion of vital behaviors, barriers, evidence of change, and lessons for the next cycle."
Key point:
- Influence capability grows through repeated thinking, discussion, and practice.
- Embed behavioral analysis in ordinary leadership routines.
44. Q: What is the most important professional lesson from Chapter 1?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is that meaningful leadership requires more than having authority, expertise, a vision, or a persuasive message. It requires a deliberate and repeatable ability to influence people to change important behaviors in ways that produce valued results. The examples of Danny Meyer and Rich Sheridan show that seemingly different performance challenges can share a human dimension: employees must perform specific actions consistently, even when doing so is difficult or uncomfortable. The research cases extend the idea across public health, organizations, communities, and personal life. Chapter 1 therefore asks leaders to stop treating behavior as an afterthought and make it a central subject of diagnosis, learning, and practice.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a short explanation of the chapter's thesis using one example from Meyer, one from Sheridan, and one example from your own work or community."
Key point:
- Leadership is the repeatable practice of influencing behavior for valued results.
- Start with the human actions that make the desired outcome possible.
Rows: 44
Table 2
The Three Keys to Effective Influence
1. Q: What are the three keys to effective influence, and how do they work together?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents three mutually reinforcing disciplines: focus and measure the results you want; identify the small number of vital behaviors that can materially improve those results; and engage all six sources of influence so people can adopt and sustain those behaviors. The sequence starts with the desired result and works backward to the behaviors and the conditions that support them. The keys are not three unrelated techniques. A clear result gives direction, vital behaviors translate the result into action, and the six-source model helps explain and change the forces that shape those actions. Omitting a key leaves the change effort without a clear destination, a practical behavioral target, or a sufficiently broad influence strategy.
Study instruction:
- "Write the three keys in sequence, then use one workplace change challenge to show what each key contributes."
Key point:
- Results provide direction; vital behaviors translate direction into action.
- The six sources create conditions in which the required actions can become consistent habits.
2. Q: Why should an influencer ask both “Why are they doing that?” and “How can I help them do this instead?”
A: Show answer
These two questions expose the theory of influence that is already guiding a person's judgments and interventions. The first is diagnostic: it seeks an explanation for current behavior. The second is strategic: it determines what the influencer will do to encourage a different behavior. If the diagnosis is incomplete, the proposed intervention is likely to address only a visible symptom or the influencer's preferred explanation. For example, interpreting a missed deadline as laziness may lead to pressure, while a broader diagnosis may reveal unclear instructions, missing skills, peer norms, or a process that makes timely completion difficult. Effective influence requires testing the explanation before selecting the intervention.
Study instruction:
- "For a behavior you want to change, write at least three possible explanations before choosing an intervention."
Key point:
- A diagnosis shapes the intervention that follows.
- Do not treat the first explanation that comes to mind as an established cause.
3. Q: How does the Crucial Influence model explain the way the world works?
A: Show answer
The model describes a causal sequence moving from left to right: six sources of influence shape choices; repeated choices become vital or routine behaviors; and those behaviors produce results, positive or negative. The sources include personal, social, and structural forces affecting motivation and ability. When these forces remain stable, they tend to reinforce existing behavior and make it normal. In the chapter's hospital example, multiple influences support poor hand hygiene, the practice becomes an organizational norm, and hospital-acquired infections increase. The model encourages leaders to look beyond an outcome and examine the behaviors and influences that produce it.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the sequence from sources of influence to behavior to results, and map a real operational problem onto each stage."
Key point:
- Results are produced by behavior, and behavior is shaped by multiple influences.
- Persistent outcomes often reflect stable conditions that reinforce repeated actions.
4. Q: Why do effective leaders work backward from results when planning change?
A: Show answer
The model's natural causal direction runs from influences to behaviors to results, but the chapter recommends planning in the reverse direction. Leaders first define the result they need, then determine which behaviors would make the greatest contribution, and finally identify how to influence those behaviors. Starting with a preferred solution or training course risks solving the wrong problem. Working backward creates a logical test for each proposed action: does it support a vital behavior, and is that behavior likely to improve the result? This sequence helps keep interventions connected to outcomes rather than allowing activities to become ends in themselves.
Study instruction:
- "Take an existing change initiative and rewrite its plan in result-first order: result, vital behavior, then supporting influences."
Key point:
- Plan from the result backward, even though results arise from influences and behaviors.
- Every intervention should have a clear connection to a required behavior and outcome.
5. Q: What makes a desired result sufficiently clear to guide an influence effort?
A: Show answer
A useful result statement identifies what is to change in concrete terms and makes success distinguishable from failure. It should communicate the outcome that matters, the people or population affected, the intended level of achievement, and a relevant time frame where one can reasonably be set. Broad aspirations such as improving service, increasing quality, or helping communities are not precise enough to guide choices because different people can interpret them differently. The chapter's examples show how a measurable target can align decisions and conversations throughout an organization. Clarity does not mean reducing a meaningful mission to a number; it means expressing the mission in a form that can guide and test action.
Study instruction:
- "Convert one broad aspiration into a result statement that identifies the intended outcome, population, measure, and time frame."
Key point:
- A clear result removes avoidable ambiguity about what success means.
- A target should preserve the mission's meaning while making progress assessable.
6. Q: Why are vague objectives weak foundations for influence?
A: Show answer
Vague objectives permit different interpretations of what people should do and how success should be judged. A call to improve customer service, for example, could mean faster telephone response, more helpful problem-solving, better product quality, or additional customer benefits. Teams may therefore work hard but pursue different outcomes. Vague goals also make it difficult to choose useful measures or determine whether an intervention is working. A clear target gives people a common reference point for prioritizing effort, coordinating decisions, and assessing progress. The chapter's examples contrast broad aspirations with specific, time-bound goals that changed how people understood their work.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a vague objective from a work plan and list three conflicting interpretations it could produce before rewriting it precisely."
Key point:
- Ambiguity fragments effort because people may pursue different definitions of success.
- Specific goals create a shared basis for action and evaluation.
7. Q: How can a leader make a result statement compelling as well as measurable?
A: Show answer
A compelling result connects a measurable target to an outcome people regard as meaningful. The chapter describes leaders who did not merely seek to improve a statistic; they framed the objective in terms of lives saved or families moving above a poverty threshold. A useful statement therefore combines precision with human significance. It explains why the outcome matters, what achievement would look like, and when progress will be assessed. The measure should not displace the purpose: the number is useful because it helps people focus on the real-world change it represents. Leaders should communicate the goal consistently so that teams can connect daily work to the broader result.
Study instruction:
- "Draft two versions of the same goal—one purely numerical and one that connects the measure to its human or organizational purpose—and compare their clarity."
Key point:
- A meaningful target engages commitment as well as attention.
- The measure should represent the intended change, not replace it.
8. Q: What can be learned from Dr. Don Berwick's 100,000 Lives Campaign example?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the campaign to demonstrate the influence of a clear, ambitious, time-bound result. Rather than asking healthcare professionals simply to reduce medical errors or improve safety, Berwick articulated a specific number of lives to save and a deadline. That formulation gave a large group a common objective that was difficult to misunderstand. It also connected the work to a consequential human outcome rather than an abstract performance indicator. The example illustrates the first key: later tactics have a stronger foundation when people first understand exactly what they are trying to accomplish. The case should be read as an illustration of goal clarity, not as a universal formula that every target must copy.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the campaign's numerical target, deadline, and human purpose made the objective more actionable than a general call to improve safety."
Key point:
- Specificity and a deadline can focus collective effort.
- An ambitious target is useful when it remains connected to a meaningful outcome.
9. Q: What does the Fundación Paraguaya example show about changing an organization's definition of success?
A: Show answer
The organization initially emphasized providing poor families with access to credit, including the number of loans processed. After many years, its leaders questioned whether that activity measure adequately represented the desired social outcome. They shifted attention to a specific target for families to reach an income level associated with the national poverty line by a defined date. The revised goal changed how employees understood their roles, what capabilities loan officers needed, and the conversations they had with clients. The example shows that an organization may need to replace a convenient output measure with an outcome that better represents its mission. A measure can shape organizational priorities, not merely report them.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the activity of delivering a service from the outcome the service is intended to produce in one organization you know."
Key point:
- Counting outputs is not the same as measuring mission impact.
- A revised result definition can reshape roles, skills, and daily decisions.
10. Q: Why does the chapter describe measurement as a way to motivate action rather than merely track performance?
A: Show answer
Measurement directs attention toward selected priorities, makes progress visible, and gives people feedback about whether their actions are helping. A progress display comparing countries' performance in Guinea worm eradication, for example, refocused a national leader who had lost attention on the campaign. The measure worked because it made relative progress salient and prompted renewed action. Measurement can also help people learn whether an intervention is effective and where additional effort is needed. It therefore has an active influence function: what leaders measure and discuss signals what matters. Measures selected only by habit or external reporting requirements may fail to create this connection.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a measure used in your team and explain what behavior it currently attracts attention to or encourages."
Key point:
- Measures influence attention, motivation, and learning.
- A measure is useful when it changes what people notice and do.
11. Q: What question should a leader ask to test whether a measure is influential?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes asking, “How does this measure influence behavior?” This question shifts attention from whether data can be collected to whether the information will help people act. A measure may be accurate and easy to report yet have little influence if the people expected to change cannot connect it to their own work. Conversely, a well-chosen measure can clarify priorities, show progress, and provide timely feedback. Leaders should consider who will see the measure, what decisions it informs, how frequently it is discussed, and whether the people responsible can affect it. If no plausible behavior or decision changes as a result, the measure may be serving reporting convenience rather than influence.
Study instruction:
- "For three existing indicators, state the behavior each is intended to influence and identify who can act on the information."
Key point:
- Data collection alone does not make a measure influential.
- A useful measure has a clear behavioral purpose.
12. Q: Why is failure to measure a change effort a serious weakness?
A: Show answer
Without measures, leaders cannot reliably distinguish actual progress from impressions, anecdotes, or optimism. The chapter identifies several reasons measurement is omitted: leaders believe they already know the situation, underestimate the role of measurement in influence, avoid accountability, or regard measurement as too much work. In each case, the effort lacks a dependable way to test whether the desired result is occurring. A willingness to measure also demonstrates that leaders are prepared to test their stated commitment. Measurement should be proportionate to the task, but resource constraints do not remove the need for credible evidence about whether the change is working.
Study instruction:
- "Review a change initiative and identify which claims of progress are supported by reliable measures and which rely only on impressions."
Key point:
- Unmeasured progress is difficult to verify or manage.
- Avoiding measurement can weaken accountability and reveal a lack of commitment.
13. Q: What is the difference between measuring a distant result and measuring a result people can influence directly?
A: Show answer
A distant result is an important outcome but may be separated from an individual's daily actions by many other factors. For example, total hospital-acquired infections across a large hospital system may be too remote for one unit's staff to see how their conduct affects the figure. A closer measure, such as hand-hygiene compliance within that unit, connects daily practice to progress toward the larger result. The distant outcome remains essential, but it should be accompanied by a measure that people can influence and use for feedback. The closer measure creates a practical link between personal contribution and the overall goal.
Study instruction:
- "For one strategic outcome, identify a local behavior or intermediate indicator that the responsible team can influence directly."
Key point:
- People are more engaged when they can see how their actions affect progress.
- Use close measures to connect daily work with important distant outcomes.
14. Q: How should a leader select a close, controllable, and motivating measure?
A: Show answer
A useful measure should satisfy three related tests. It should be close enough to the people whose behavior needs to change that they can see the connection to their work; controllable enough that their actions can affect it; and motivating because it is meaningfully predictive of the result being pursued. The chapter's discussion of the US Army's prevention efforts illustrates the search for a variable that local commanders could influence and that could help prevent the serious outcome. The measure may concern beliefs, attitudes, or behaviors, but it should not be chosen merely because it is easy to count. Leaders should be able to explain why improvement in the measure is expected to contribute to the desired result.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the three tests—closeness, controllability, and predictive relevance—to a proposed indicator before adopting it."
Key point:
- A strong measure connects local action to an important result.
- Ease of measurement is not enough; the indicator must be relevant and actionable.
15. Q: What does the hospital hand-hygiene example teach about leading and lagging measures?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts the ultimate result—reducing hospital-acquired infections—with a closer measure of hand-hygiene compliance by hospital unit. Infection rates show whether the larger outcome is improving, but they may not provide immediate feedback to each frontline team. Compliance measurement gives teams a more direct view of the practice they can change. The chapter does not require a particular technical classification of indicators, but the example illustrates the practical distinction between measuring an eventual outcome and measuring a behavior that contributes to it. Both views are useful: one confirms whether the result is achieved, while the other helps guide day-to-day action.
Study instruction:
- "Create a two-level measurement example for a safety or quality objective: one outcome measure and one behavior measure."
Key point:
- Outcome measures show whether the goal is being achieved.
- Behavior measures help teams see what they can change now.
16. Q: What is the lesson from the US Army example about measuring prevention?
A: Show answer
The Army initially relied on the total number of reported sexual assaults across the entire institution. That measure was too distant for many local commanders to feel direct responsibility, and a low reported count in one unit did not necessarily prove that the unit had a safe climate. The chapter describes a shift toward asking whether personnel trusted that reports would be investigated properly and whether peers would intervene to prevent assault or harassment. These questions were closer to the influence of local commanders and focused on conditions that could support prevention. The example demonstrates why a measure should reflect the behavior or climate that leaders can shape, while avoiding the mistaken assumption that a low incident count alone proves success.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why incident counts alone may be insufficient for assessing a local prevention effort, and propose a closer measure of protective behavior or confidence."
Key point:
- A low count does not automatically demonstrate that prevention conditions are strong.
- Measure the local conditions and behaviors that can help prevent the harmful result.
17. Q: Why must measures be refreshed frequently enough to sustain attention?
A: Show answer
A measure that is reviewed too rarely may fail to keep the desired change visible in people's daily priorities. The chapter compares this with a sporting event in which the score is announced only at the end: participants receive too little feedback to stay engaged during the activity. Frequent measurement can make progress visible, reinforce attention, and help people adjust their actions before the final outcome is known. The appropriate frequency depends on the behavior, the time needed for meaningful change, and the cost of collecting reliable information. The objective is not to measure everything constantly, but to review important indicators often enough to inform action and sustain focus.
Study instruction:
- "Set a realistic review frequency for one change indicator and explain how the timing will support decisions rather than create reporting activity."
Key point:
- Infrequent feedback can allow attention to drift.
- Measure often enough to support timely learning and action.
18. Q: What does the blood-pressure monitoring example illustrate about frequent feedback?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes patients who were not consistently taking heart medication and a suggestion to let them monitor their blood pressure twice daily. Seeing progress associated with their medication compliance helped sustain attention and was followed by higher compliance. The example illustrates how a frequent, understandable measure can make an otherwise distant health objective more immediate to the individual. It also provides feedback about whether the person's actions appear to be helping. The lesson is not that every behavior should be monitored twice daily; it is that the frequency and form of feedback should fit the behavior and make progress visible to the people expected to act.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a behavior-change example and determine what feedback an individual could receive frequently enough to see the effect of their actions."
Key point:
- Frequent feedback can connect routine actions with visible progress.
- Measurement frequency should fit the behavior and decision cycle.
19. Q: How can the frequency of measurement influence which priorities receive management attention?
A: Show answer
Managers naturally spend time reviewing the information that is regularly presented and discussed. The chapter notes that organizations may review budgets, sales, and production weekly or monthly while assessing culture only annually. In that situation, financial and operational metrics repeatedly enter management conversations, whereas culture receives attention briefly after the annual survey. The difference in review frequency can therefore influence which issues remain prominent. Leaders who want behavior or culture change to matter must give its measures an appropriate place in routine management discussions, not simply announce that the topic is important.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the review frequency of financial, operational, people, and culture indicators in one organization and identify which priorities dominate management conversations."
Key point:
- Review frequency is a signal of organizational priority.
- Important behavioral outcomes need a regular place in management attention.
20. Q: Why does the chapter state that measurement is part of influence rather than a separate administrative activity?
A: Show answer
Measurement is part of influence because it shapes what people notice, discuss, value, and adjust. Treating measurement as an additional reporting burden separates it from the behavior-change strategy and makes it easier to neglect. The chapter argues that a measure helps keep the mission visible and tells participants whether their influence approach is working. A good measurement system is therefore designed alongside the intervention: it identifies what progress should look like, who can affect it, and when feedback is needed. This does not mean collecting data without purpose; it means integrating purposeful feedback into the work of changing behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Redesign one change initiative so that its measures are embedded in team routines and linked to decisions or adjustments."
Key point:
- Measurement is an active component of the change strategy.
- Build feedback into the work instead of treating it as a detached reporting exercise.
21. Q: What are the three measurement mistakes highlighted in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three recurring mistakes: taking no measures, measuring an outcome too distant from the people expected to act, and measuring too infrequently to maintain attention. These errors weaken influence in different ways. Without measures, leaders cannot verify progress. With only distant measures, frontline participants may not see how their actions matter. With infrequent measures, feedback arrives too late or too rarely to sustain attention and guide adjustments. A sound approach addresses all three by selecting meaningful indicators, connecting them to controllable behavior, and reviewing them at a useful cadence.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a current change effort against the three mistakes and specify one corrective action for each weakness found."
Key point:
- Measure something meaningful, close enough to influence, and often enough to guide action.
- A single headline indicator rarely provides all the feedback a change effort needs.
22. Q: What are vital behaviors in the Crucial Influence model?
A: Show answer
Vital behaviors are the small number of specific actions that can disproportionately improve the results an organization or individual wants. They translate a broad outcome into something people can actually do and repeat. The chapter emphasizes that influence is ultimately about changing behavior, not merely producing favorable feelings or agreement. Examples include lifeguards scanning their assigned pool area at defined intervals, community members filtering drinking water correctly, and peers taking responsibility for their own conduct and helping others improve. A vital behavior is not simply a slogan or a desirable attitude; it is an action whose consistent practice can materially affect the result.
Study instruction:
- "For one result you care about, identify one or two concrete actions that people would need to perform consistently."
Key point:
- Vital behaviors turn desired outcomes into observable action.
- A small number of high-leverage behaviors can produce disproportionate results.
23. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish behavior change from changing hearts and minds?
A: Show answer
The chapter treats feelings and thoughts as important influences on action, but not as the final objective of an influence effort. A person may agree with a safety message, feel committed to customer service, or express support for a new policy without changing what they do. The desired end is a changed action—such as washing hands, intervening when someone is at risk, or consistently performing a required practice. Feelings, beliefs, and understanding matter because they can support the behavior, but they should not be mistaken for evidence that the behavior has changed. Leaders should therefore define the actions they expect to see and observe whether those actions occur.
Study instruction:
- "Take a change objective framed as awareness or commitment and rewrite it as a specific action that could be observed."
Key point:
- Attitudes can support change, but behavior is the practical test.
- Do not equate agreement with consistent action.
24. Q: Why can one or two vital behaviors be more useful than a long list of desired actions?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that even complex problems often have a small number of behaviors that account for a large share of the possible improvement. A long list can dilute attention, make practice harder to sustain, and obscure which actions matter most. Selecting a few high-leverage behaviors gives people a clearer target and allows leaders to focus training, feedback, social support, and environmental changes. The selection must be evidence-informed rather than arbitrary: the behavior should plausibly influence the result and be practicable in the real setting. The aim is not to ignore other necessary work, but to identify the actions that deserve concentrated influence effort.
Study instruction:
- "Reduce a broad behavior list to the one or two actions most likely to affect the result, and justify the selection."
Key point:
