- Correction should protect dignity and focus on behavior, not personal worth.
4. Q: What did Stanley Milgram's obedience experiments examine?
A: Show answer
Milgram examined how ordinary people respond when an apparent authority figure directs them to perform actions that conflict with their concern for another person. In the experiment described in the chapter, a participant acting as a teacher believed that increasingly strong electric shocks were being delivered to a learner after incorrect answers. The learner's protests escalated, and the researcher in a lab coat repeatedly instructed the teacher to continue. The learner was not actually shocked; the distressing responses were prerecorded. Milgram was investigating the power of authority and social pressure, not testing the participants' ability to teach word pairs. The study illustrates how a structured social situation can influence behavior even when individuals experience visible discomfort.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the roles of the teacher, learner, and researcher, and distinguish what the participant believed from what actually happened.
Key point:
- The apparent authority figure and experimental setting shaped participants' choices.
- The learner's shocks were simulated, although participants believed them to be real.
5. Q: Which features of the Milgram experiment made the social pressure especially strong?
A: Show answer
Several features combined to make refusal difficult. Participants were recruited for a legitimate-sounding learning study, met a friendly fellow participant who was actually a research confederate, and were assigned the teacher role through a procedure that secretly guaranteed that outcome. The researcher wore a lab coat and presented the procedure as scientifically authorized. Participants received a painful 45-volt sample shock themselves, making the machine seem credible. The learner's protests grew more urgent, but the researcher used standardized prompts to insist that the experiment continue. These features created a conflict between personal concern and the apparent legitimacy, authority, and expectations of the situation.
Study instruction:
- List the situational cues that could make a participant defer to the researcher despite personal discomfort.
Key point:
- Authority, procedural legitimacy, and social expectations can reinforce one another.
- People may comply even when their behavior conflicts with their expressed concern.
6. Q: What results from Milgram's study are emphasized in the chapter, and what do they suggest?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that psychologists predicted only about 1.2 percent of participants would deliver the maximum shock, whereas 65 percent of the actual subjects continued to 450 volts. In another version, a confederate acting as a fellow teacher appeared to comply readily, and 90 percent of subjects reached the maximum. These figures are presented to show how strongly the presence and behavior of others can affect an individual's actions. The peer condition is particularly striking: a fellow participant's apparent compliance increased the pressure to continue. The results warn leaders that people may follow a social cue even when it conflicts with their own unease.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between the predicted result, the reported baseline result, and the peer-confederate result.
Key point:
- The chapter reports 65 percent reaching 450 volts in the main condition and 90 percent in the peer-compliance condition.
- A peer's behavior can amplify the influence of an authority figure.
7. Q: What are the limits of the lesson that should be drawn from Milgram's experiment?
A: Show answer
The experiment demonstrates the influence of a particular authority-based laboratory situation; it does not prove that every person will always obey every authority or that the reported percentages apply to all workplaces and cultures. Participants were responding within a staged experiment with specific instructions, expectations, and cues. The chapter uses the findings as a warning about human vulnerability to social influence, not as a complete explanation of historical atrocities or a universal prediction of behavior. Leaders should therefore use the study to examine the pressures built into their own systems, while avoiding simplistic labels such as 'bad people' or assumptions that one study explains every form of compliance.
Study instruction:
- When discussing the experiment, separate what the chapter reports from broader conclusions that the experiment alone cannot establish.
Key point:
- Use the experiment as a warning about situational influence, not as a universal rule about every person.
- Avoid turning a complex historical and ethical issue into a simplistic personality explanation.
8. Q: What three lessons about social influence organize the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three connected lessons. First, social influence starts with the leader: the person asking for change must model the vital behavior and demonstrate credibility through action. Second, the pace of change depends on how quickly formal leaders and opinion leaders join the effort; their visible support helps make innovation acceptable to others. Third, peer accountability accelerates change because norms become stronger when people consistently encourage the right behavior and challenge violations. The three lessons operate together: leaders establish credibility, influential people help the behavior spread, and peers sustain it in daily practice.
Study instruction:
- Use the three lessons as headings when diagnosing a change initiative and identify evidence for each one.
Key point:
- Model the behavior, enlist influential people, and build peer accountability.
- A change effort is stronger when these social mechanisms reinforce one another.
9. Q: Why must a leader 'walk the talk' before asking others to change?
A: Show answer
People assess whether the leader's actions support the stated message. When a requested behavior is unfamiliar, risky, or personally costly, employees look to the leader for cues about whether the invitation is genuine. If the leader asks for candor but reacts defensively to criticism, the reaction is stronger evidence than the speech. If the leader asks staff to prioritize safety but tolerates shortcuts, the stated value loses credibility. The chapter argues that influence begins with the leader's own conduct because people may interpret ambiguous actions in ways that confirm existing mistrust. Consistent modeling reduces the gap between what the leader says and what employees believe the organization truly rewards.
Study instruction:
- Compare a leader's stated value with three observable actions that either support or contradict it.
Key point:
- People judge the credibility of change messages through the leader's behavior.
- Visible consistency makes it safer for others to adopt difficult behaviors.
10. Q: How did Ken's response to Shirley's feedback demonstrate social influence?
A: Show answer
Ken, a chief executive trying to create a more candid culture, publicly acknowledged that employees considered him unapproachable and invited feedback from his senior managers. Shirley accepted the risk of responding. Ken then met with her privately, listened, and—with her permission—shared the feedback with the wider group. He thanked her sincerely, committed to specific changes, and followed through. This sequence made the desired behavior credible: the leader asked for candor, accepted it without retaliation, and changed his own conduct. The story also became a vicarious example for the other managers, showing them what could happen when someone spoke honestly to a senior leader.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the sequence from Ken's invitation through his follow-through and identify where trust could have been lost.
Key point:
- The invitation mattered because Ken accepted and acted on the feedback.
- A visible example can influence observers who were not part of the original conversation.
11. Q: Why was Shirley's role important even though Ken was the formal leader?
A: Show answer
Shirley supplied a credible peer example. Her willingness to raise suggestions demonstrated that a respected employee could take the risk of speaking candidly. Ken's response then showed that the leader would support rather than punish that behavior. The chapter identifies three reinforcing elements in the incident: the leader led, an opinion leader helped, and the pair created a memorable vicarious experience for the other managers. Shirley's influence was not a replacement for Ken's responsibility; it helped translate the leader's invitation into a socially observable action that others could imagine repeating.
Study instruction:
- Identify the formal leader and the opinion leader in a change scenario, then describe the distinct contribution each makes.
Key point:
- Formal authority can open the door, while a respected peer makes the behavior socially credible.
- Neither role should be treated as a substitute for the other.
12. Q: How can a manager make it safer for employees to disclose failures and bad news?
A: Show answer
A manager can model the behavior first by sharing accurate examples of setbacks, mistakes, and lessons without sugarcoating them. The nurse manager in the chapter began meetings with the good, the bad, and the ugly, usually starting with her own observations. When staff shared problems, she responded with appreciation for noticing and reporting them, then used the information to improve care. This communicates that reporting a problem is a contribution rather than an act of disloyalty. The manager must also distinguish honest disclosure from negligence or misconduct: learning from a failure does not mean ignoring accountability or patient-safety requirements.
Study instruction:
- Design a short team-meeting routine in which the manager shares one learning and invites staff to surface one risk or setback.
Key point:
- Leaders normalize candor by demonstrating it themselves and responding constructively.
- Reported problems should lead to learning and action, not automatic blame.
13. Q: Why may employees distrust a leader's request for a new behavior?
A: Show answer
Employees interpret a request through their history with the leader, the organization's past practices, and the personal risks of complying. In the Siddiqui example, staff were asked to speak up regardless of rank, but they worked in a hierarchical cultural setting and associated management with difficult wage conditions. They suspected the invitation might be a trick because it required employees to take risks without an equivalent change from management. When people already distrust a leader, they may interpret ambiguous actions as confirmation of their concerns. The leader must therefore provide clear, repeated, observable evidence that the new behavior is genuinely supported.
Study instruction:
- List the historical experiences and practical risks that might cause a team to doubt a new leadership message.
Key point:
- A request does not erase the history that shapes how it is interpreted.
- Credibility must be demonstrated through actions employees can observe.
14. Q: Why does the chapter describe sacrifice as an accelerant of trust?
A: Show answer
A sacrifice makes a leader's stated values more credible because it shows that the leader is willing to bear a real cost to act consistently with them. Employees can discount a speech or interpret a symbolic gesture as public relations, but a meaningful choice involving time, money, ego, or competing priorities is harder to dismiss. Siddiqui listened, apologized, and spent weekends visiting employees' homes; these actions challenged expectations about hierarchy and status. The chapter does not suggest that one dramatic gesture permanently earns trust. The evidence must be sincere, relevant to the value being promoted, repeated where necessary, and followed by consistent conduct.
Study instruction:
- Choose one value a leader wants others to adopt and identify a genuine personal cost that would demonstrate commitment to it.
Key point:
- Sacrifice converts an abstract value into observable evidence.
- A one-off performance cannot replace sustained consistency.
15. Q: How can sacrificing time demonstrate that a leader values people?
A: Show answer
Time is a finite resource, so the amount and quality of attention a leader gives can communicate whether the issue and the people involved matter. Siddiqui's visits to employees' homes were significant partly because he stayed for about an hour over tea rather than making a brief five-minute appearance. The longer visit allowed conversation, listening, and respect to be demonstrated in practice. The lesson is not that every leader must conduct home visits; it is that the form of attention should be proportionate to the importance of the relationship and the message. Rushed, ceremonial contact may contradict a claim that people are a priority.
Study instruction:
- Compare the message sent by a brief courtesy visit with the message sent by a protected, attentive conversation.
Key point:
- Time is a visible and limited resource that can signal genuine priority.
- Quality of attention matters more than a staged display of busyness.
16. Q: How can a financial sacrifice make a service value believable?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the authors' experience with Hertz after the September 11 attacks. With air travel grounded, they needed to drive a rental car from Dallas to Salt Lake City to reach their families. The agent waived the one-way drop-off fee and told them to return the car wherever convenient. The company incurred a short-term financial cost to serve customers during a crisis, making its customer-service message credible. The transferable principle is that a value becomes persuasive when the organization accepts a real trade-off in order to honor it. Such decisions still need appropriate authority and financial controls; a service gesture should not bypass lawful approval or create unsustainable commitments.
Study instruction:
- Identify a customer or employee situation where honoring a stated value would require a measurable short-term cost, then identify who can authorize it.
Key point:
- A real trade-off can provide stronger evidence than advertising or slogans.
- Financial generosity should remain authorized, proportionate, and sustainable.
17. Q: How can admitting a mistake and sacrificing ego strengthen a leader's credibility?
A: Show answer
The chapter's example of Liz shows that a leader can recover credibility by publicly acknowledging conduct that contradicted her stated values. After dismissing an employee's complaint about a missed facility tour, Liz recognized that she had damaged trust. At the next briefing she stepped forward, described her behavior as unacceptable, apologized, asked forgiveness, and committed not to repeat it. The apology had influence because she placed integrity above the need to appear right. An effective apology names the conduct, acknowledges its effect, avoids excuses, and is followed by changed behavior. It should not be used as a substitute for correcting harm or addressing the underlying issue.
Study instruction:
- Draft an apology that names a specific action, acknowledges its impact, accepts responsibility, and states a verifiable corrective commitment.
Key point:
- Admitting a mistake can make a stated value more credible than pretending to be flawless.
- An apology earns trust through ownership and subsequent behavior.
18. Q: What does sacrificing other priorities mean in the Dain Hancock example?
A: Show answer
Dain Hancock, CEO of Lockheed Martin Aeronautics, had been perceived by some employees as caring more about external image than employee concerns. During a 90-minute employee feedback meeting, he was told that a visiting prince had arrived two hours early for a discussion about a major order. Rather than end the employee session, Dain assigned his COO to greet the prince and continued listening to staff. The choice quickly spread through the facility and changed how employees interpreted his priorities. The lesson is that a leader's calendar and decisions reveal what truly takes precedence. A competing priority should be set aside only when doing so is responsible and within the leader's authority.
Study instruction:
- Identify a visible competing demand that could test whether a leader genuinely prioritizes the value being promoted.
Key point:
- Priorities become credible when leaders make consistent choices under pressure.
- Employees notice what leaders protect when demands conflict.
19. Q: How should a leader choose a sacrifice that is credible rather than performative?
A: Show answer
The sacrifice should be directly connected to the behavior the leader is asking others to adopt, meaningful to the people affected, proportionate to the situation, and sustainable enough to be reinforced by later conduct. A symbolic gesture unrelated to the real barrier may appear manipulative. For example, asking employees to speak up while refusing to hear criticism does not demonstrate sacrifice; inviting candid feedback and changing one's own behavior does. Leaders should avoid using personal generosity to create dependency or to evade fair policy. The strongest evidence is a series of consistent choices that remove a real cost or risk from the people being asked to change.
Study instruction:
- Test a proposed leadership gesture against relevance, visibility, proportionality, consistency, and whether it addresses the actual barrier.
Key point:
- Credible sacrifice is connected to the desired behavior and experienced as genuine.
- Performative gestures without follow-through can deepen cynicism.
20. Q: What role do formal leaders play in a social change effort?
A: Show answer
Formal leaders influence behavior through their authority, visibility, resource decisions, expectations, and daily interactions with employees. The chapter recommends engaging the chain of command and creating specific plans for managers to teach, model, praise, and hold people accountable for the vital behaviors. Their support helps make innovation safe and legitimate. If managers remain silent, contradict the initiative, or continue rewarding the old behavior, employees receive mixed signals. Formal sponsorship should therefore be translated into observable routines, not left as a one-time endorsement at a launch meeting.
Study instruction:
- Map the formal management chain for a proposed change and specify what each level should teach, model, recognize, and review.
Key point:
- Formal leaders make the change visible and institutionally supported.
- A title or announcement alone does not demonstrate day-to-day commitment.
21. Q: How can a change leader create a practical engagement plan for formal managers?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying the specific vital behaviors each management level can influence. Define what managers should explain, demonstrate, recognize, correct, and monitor, and specify how often those actions should occur. Give managers examples and practice opportunities so that expectations are concrete. Provide a channel for them to raise implementation barriers and ensure that their own managers review whether the commitments are being met. The plan should distinguish supportive coaching from formal performance management and should not ask managers to enforce a behavior they have not been equipped or authorized to support.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page manager action plan with a behavior, a visible leadership action, a frequency, and evidence of completion.
Key point:
- Manager engagement becomes useful when translated into repeatable actions.
- Training, authority, and follow-up must support the requested leadership behavior.
22. Q: Who are opinion leaders, and how do they differ from formal leaders?
A: Show answer
Opinion leaders are people who are admired, respected, and connected within a social group and whose judgments influence how others interpret an idea or practice. They may have no formal title or supervisory authority. Formal leaders influence through an assigned role and decision rights; opinion leaders influence through trust, credibility, relationships, and informal communication. The chapter stresses that opinion leaders are already observing a change strategy and will communicate approval or disapproval whether or not the organization formally engages them. A successful effort identifies and partners with them rather than assuming that authority alone will secure adoption.
Study instruction:
- Identify one formal leader and one informal opinion leader in a group, and explain the different sources of influence each holds.
Key point:
- Formal authority and informal credibility are distinct sources of social influence.
- Opinion leaders shape peer judgments even when they hold no official role.
23. Q: What is the difference between innovators and opinion leaders in the chapter's discussion of Everett Rogers?
A: Show answer
Innovators are people who are especially open to trying new ideas early, often before the wider community is ready. Opinion leaders, described in the chapter as early adopters, are also receptive to innovation but are distinguished by being respected and socially connected within the group. The chapter reports that this opinion-leader group represents about 13.5 percent of a population and argues that the broader majority is unlikely to adopt until these respected people do. The distinction matters because the first person willing to try an idea may not be able to persuade others. Adoption depends not only on enthusiasm for an innovation but also on the social credibility of the messenger.
Study instruction:
- Compare an enthusiastic first adopter with a respected, well-connected colleague and assess which one the wider group is more likely to follow.
Key point:
- Being first to adopt is not the same as being influential.
- Social credibility and connection help an innovation cross into the wider group.
24. Q: What did Everett Rogers learn from the Iowa corn-seed example?
A: Show answer
Rogers was asked to encourage farmers to use improved corn strains that promised better yields and disease resistance. His university expertise did not persuade farmers because he lacked practical farming experience and was seen as an outsider. He then found an innovative farmer who tried the seeds and obtained a good crop, but the community did not follow. The farmer was visibly different from his peers and lacked the respect and connections needed to influence the wider group. Rogers' failed attempt helped him focus on how innovations move through social systems and why the identity of the messenger can matter as much as the merit of the idea.
Study instruction:
- Explain why the improved seed and a successful early trial were insufficient to create wider adoption in the story.
Key point:
- A strong idea can fail to spread when its messenger lacks social credibility.
- The first adopter may not be the person who persuades the majority.
25. Q: Why does the chapter say the message is no more important than the messenger?
A: Show answer
People evaluate information partly through their trust in the person delivering it. A technically sound message may be rejected if it comes from someone viewed as an outsider, while a respected peer can make an unfamiliar behavior seem credible and safe to try. The chapter illustrates this through physicians who listen to trusted professional associations, village members selected by respected local leaders, and the Carter Center's use of village chiefs and respected officials when addressing Guinea worm disease. This does not mean expertise is irrelevant; rather, evidence and messenger credibility need to work together. A credible messenger should be able to explain the behavior accurately and model it responsibly.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed change, identify the people the target group already trusts and determine whether they can accurately explain and demonstrate the behavior.
Key point:
- A persuasive message needs a messenger the audience regards as credible.
- Credibility should complement evidence, not replace it.
26. Q: How can opinion leaders help a change spread across a large organization?
A: Show answer
Opinion leaders can translate a general initiative into trusted peer conversations, answer practical questions, model the new behavior, identify local concerns, and signal whether the change is accepted by respected members of the group. The chapter suggests that a leader of a 10,000-person organization should focus on the relatively small number of influential people who shape wider opinion; in a team of 20, perhaps two or three people have disproportionate influence. The point is not to create an exclusive elite but to use limited leadership attention where it can help the new behavior travel through existing relationships.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple influence map showing how a message could travel from a small group of respected employees to the wider organization.
Key point:
- A relatively small number of connected people can have broad influence.
- Peer-to-peer communication can extend a change effort beyond formal announcements.
27. Q: How can an organization identify its opinion leaders systematically?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends asking employees to name the people they consider most influential and respected, then consolidating the nominations and identifying those who appear most frequently. This approach uses the group's own knowledge of its social network rather than relying only on job titles or a manager's personal impressions. A careful process should allow nominations across departments, shifts, locations, and relevant subgroups so that less visible communities are not overlooked. The resulting list is a starting point for validation, not a permanent ranking of personal worth. Influence can vary by topic and change over time.
Study instruction:
- Design a confidential nomination process and specify how you would include different teams, locations, and employee groups.
Key point:
- Ask the group who it respects and listens to instead of assuming influence follows hierarchy.
- Repeated nominations help reveal informal networks, but the list should be checked for coverage and bias.
28. Q: What should leaders do after identifying opinion leaders?
A: Show answer
Leaders should spend time with them, listen to their concerns, build a credible relationship, invite their ideas, and ask them to help teach and model the new behavior. The chapter's examples show that engagement is more than asking respected people to endorse a finished plan. Jyoti Desai invited opinion leaders from across business units to a retreat with her management team, created space for candid feedback about executive behavior, and secured a signed leadership contract committing executives to six changes. She then asked the opinion leaders to teach the new way of working to others. This partnership gave them a meaningful role in shaping and spreading the change.
Study instruction:
- Plan an engagement session in which opinion leaders can challenge the proposal and influence leaders' own commitments before being asked to advocate the change.
Key point:
- Opinion leaders should be partners in the change, not merely decorative endorsers.
- Listening to their concerns improves both credibility and implementation.
29. Q: How did Jyoti Desai use opinion leaders to reduce organizational silos?
A: Show answer
Desai wanted greater collaboration across business units. She identified respected people from each area and brought them together with the management team for a three-day retreat. A central exercise was a candid 90-minute discussion in which opinion leaders asked executives what they would need to change if they expected others to break down silos. Executives received direct feedback and created a leadership contract listing six behaviors they would change. The executives signed the commitments and asked the opinion leaders to teach the new way of working. The example shows that change spreads more credibly when senior leaders accept feedback and influential peers become active partners.
Study instruction:
- Extract the key design features of the retreat: cross-unit representation, candid feedback, executive commitments, visible signature, and peer teaching.
Key point:
- Leaders demonstrated willingness to change before asking others to collaborate differently.
- Opinion leaders gained a concrete role in spreading the new behavior.
30. Q: How can mentors act as opinion leaders in a service organization?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a restaurant server whose thoughtful response to a guest became a hospitality legend. Danny Meyer invited the server to become a mentor because the employee embodied the organization's service values and was respected for the way he worked. Mentors were then paired with newer employees to teach the organization's values and norms. This uses a credible practitioner to translate abstract expectations into observable examples. Effective mentors should be selected for consistent behavior and their ability to coach others, not simply for seniority or popularity. They also need clear expectations and support so that their influence reinforces the intended standard.
Study instruction:
- Identify the observable behaviors that would qualify an employee to mentor a new colleague, then design a practical mentoring assignment.
Key point:
- Respected practitioners can teach norms through example and everyday coaching.
- Mentor selection should reflect demonstrated behavior, not title alone.
31. Q: How can fictional characters or stories influence social norms?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that people can form strong emotional connections with fictional characters and use their experiences to reconsider real-life practices. In the Indian community example, the drama Tinka, Tinka Sukh portrayed a beloved young girl who died in childbirth after being forced into an early marriage. The story moved listeners and viewers to discuss the issue, write letters, and publicly express support for educating girls and delaying marriage. Each episode also ended with a respected real community member asking questions and encouraging discussion. Stories can make a difficult issue discussable without beginning with a direct lecture that may trigger resistance.
Study instruction:
- Choose a sensitive issue and outline a fictional scenario that allows people to consider its consequences without presenting a simplistic sermon.
Key point:
- A vicarious experience can help people imagine consequences and reconsider assumptions.
- Stories are more effective when they lead into real discussion and action.
32. Q: What happened in the Lutsaan example, and what mechanism drove the change?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a community in Lutsaan, India, responding to the serial drama Tinka, Tinka Sukh, which addressed early marriage and girls' education. After an episode in which a young girl died in childbirth, the program received more than 150,000 letters, and 184 villagers placed thumbprints on a public poster honoring the character. A community action group made a covenant to educate their daughters. The authors attribute the influence partly to moving an undiscussable issue into public conversation: viewers first reflected on the story, then discussed it with family, coworkers, neighbors, and friends. A respected community voice at the end of each episode further encouraged public discourse.
Study instruction:
- Trace the movement from private emotional response to public discussion and then to a community commitment.
Key point:
- The story helped make a sensitive social issue discussable.
- A respected local voice connected the fictional experience to real community action.
33. Q: What is peer accountability, and why does it accelerate change?
A: Show answer
Peer accountability means that people do more than practice the desired behavior themselves: they also encourage, correct, and remind one another when the behavior is or is not being practiced. The chapter calls this 200 percent accountability. It accelerates change because norms are sustained through repeated interactions; when peers consistently defend the new standard, the behavior becomes part of what the group expects. Leaders cannot rely only on policies or formal supervision to change a widely shared norm. Peer accountability extends reinforcement into the everyday situations where behavior actually occurs.
Study instruction:
- For one vital behavior, identify what an individual must do personally and what peers should do when they observe a deviation.
Key point:
- New norms strengthen when people actively defend them.
- Peer accountability extends influence beyond the formal management chain.
34. Q: How do unhealthy norms survive through a culture of silence?
A: Show answer
An unhealthy norm can persist when people privately recognize harmful behavior but believe it is safer or more acceptable not to discuss it. Silence protects the status quo because violations are not challenged, affected people receive no support, and observers infer that the behavior is tolerated. The chapter's healthcare example shows how deference to powerful colleagues displaced the priority of patient safety. In project teams, people anticipated failure but felt unable to discuss weak sponsorship, unrealistic constraints, or uncommitted members. The silence itself becomes a social expectation: people learn that raising the issue is more dangerous than allowing the problem to continue.
Study instruction:
- Identify what people may privately know, what they say publicly, and what social penalty they fear if they speak.
Key point:
- Silence can function as an active mechanism that protects an unhealthy norm.
- A problem may persist because discussing it is socially costly, not because nobody sees it.
35. Q: What does the hospital infection example reveal about social norms and safety?
A: Show answer
In a multiyear study described in the chapter, clinicians reported that some physicians did not consistently gown, glove, or wash as required, while some nurses cut a hole in sterile gloves to expose a fingertip when starting an IV on a very small baby. The nurses' practical difficulty did not remove the infection risk. The deeper organizational problem was that colleagues often stayed silent when hygiene and safety protocols were violated, particularly when the person involved held power. The existing norm favored deference over patient protection. The chapter argues that safety practice must include a social norm of speaking up, not merely written protocols.
Study instruction:
- Use the example to distinguish the unsafe act, the practical pressure behind it, and the silence that allowed it to continue.
Key point:
- Safety protocols are weakened when colleagues do not challenge violations.
- Speaking up must become a normal part of protecting patients.
36. Q: What do the chapter's project-management figures illustrate about organizational silence?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that roughly 90 percent of major projects violate their own schedule, budget, or quality standards. In the study described, 88 percent of respondents were working on initiatives they predicted would fail, yet the work continued. Fewer than one in ten said it was politically acceptable to speak openly about what was going wrong. These figures are presented to illustrate a gap between private recognition of risk and public discussion. They should be treated as figures reported in the chapter's study, not as universal statistics for every project portfolio. The management lesson is to create conditions where risks such as weak sponsorship, unrealistic constraints, and low commitment can be raised early.
Study instruction:
- Discuss a project you know and identify whether team members can safely raise bad news about schedule, budget, quality, sponsorship, and commitment.
Key point:
- Private awareness of failure does not help if concerns cannot be voiced.
- A speak-up norm is part of effective project governance.
37. Q: How can leaders make an undiscussable issue discussable?
A: Show answer
Leaders need to name both the existing norm and the new norm in public, provide a safe way to examine the issue, and invite evidence and perspectives from people affected by it. The chapter's examples use stories, patient accounts, candid meetings, and respected narrators to bring hidden concerns into public discourse. A practical process is to define the behavior without labeling people, explain why the topic matters, share credible examples, ask open questions, establish respectful discussion rules, and clarify how concerns will be acted on. Simply announcing that silence is unacceptable is insufficient if people still fear retaliation or see no response to what they report.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a discussion plan for a sensitive issue that includes a neutral description, relevant evidence, safe participation, and a clear follow-up route.
Key point:
- Norm change begins when the old and new expectations can be discussed openly.
- Public discussion needs credible evidence and a response process.
38. Q: How did patient stories help leaders break silence in an academic hospital?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a chief medical officer who received a stack of 50 patient horror stories about failures in care. She read them over a weekend and was emotionally affected. Beginning the following Monday, stories that had previously been whispered became widely shared, read, studied, and debated. The stories gave concrete human meaning to a quality problem that had been difficult to acknowledge publicly. The example shows how carefully gathered accounts can help leaders confront a normalized problem and make it discussable. Patient stories should be handled ethically, with privacy protected and facts verified; emotional force does not remove the need for accuracy.
Study instruction:
- Consider how anonymized, verified service-user experiences could help a team examine a recurring quality problem without breaching confidentiality.
Key point:
- Concrete stories can expose the human consequences of normalized failures.
- Use stories ethically and connect them to evidence and corrective action.
39. Q: What does '200 percent accountability' mean?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses 200 percent accountability to mean that everyone is responsible both for practicing the vital behaviors personally and for holding others accountable for practicing them. It is more than individual compliance: each person helps maintain the shared norm by encouraging the right behavior and challenging violations. The Other Side Academy illustrates this through structured peer feedback in daily life. The phrase does not mean unlimited authority to police colleagues or to impose punishment. In a workplace, it must be translated into clear behavioral expectations, respectful correction, appropriate escalation, and boundaries that protect dignity and due process.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between being accountable for your own conduct and helping a peer uphold the same standard.
Key point:
- Everyone owns the behavior and the shared expectation around it.
- Peer accountability must operate within clear and respectful boundaries.
40. Q: What are the three peer-accountability practices used at The Other Side Academy?
A: Show answer
Residents were expected to do three things: pull others up, pass information, and play The Game. A 'pull up' is a brief verbal correction from one person to another when a norm is violated. Passing information means notifying the immediate supervisor after either giving or receiving a correction, so that relevant information reaches the peer leader. The Game is a twice-weekly, two-hour group feedback process in which residents discuss concerns in greater depth and help one another recognize and address weaknesses. Together, these practices combine immediate feedback, communication to a responsible leader, and a structured forum for more substantial reflection.
Study instruction:
- Describe when a brief correction is appropriate, when information should be passed to a supervisor, and when a fuller group discussion is needed.
Key point:
- Immediate correction, information sharing, and structured feedback serve different purposes.
- A peer-accountability system needs both informal and formalized channels.
41. Q: How do praise and corrective feedback work together in a strong peer culture?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a social system that uses both praise for desired behavior and correction when people violate expectations. At The Other Side Academy, residents were publicly celebrated when they advanced, while peers also challenged behavior such as disrespect, dishonesty, isolation, or failing to contribute fairly. Praise helps people recognize what to repeat and signals that progress is valued. Corrective feedback makes the boundaries of the norm visible. A balanced system should not allow correction to dominate so heavily that people become fearful or hide mistakes. Recognition and correction should be specific, timely, proportionate, and linked to the agreed standard.
Study instruction:
- Create two examples of feedback for the same vital behavior: one recognizing compliance and one correcting a specific deviation.
Key point:
- Praise teaches the group what to repeat; correction clarifies what must change.
- A balanced feedback culture reinforces learning as well as accountability.
42. Q: How can peer correction be made constructive rather than humiliating?
A: Show answer
A constructive correction refers to an observable action, identifies the relevant expectation, explains the impact, and gives the person an opportunity to respond and correct course. It should be timely but not impulsive, delivered in a setting appropriate to the seriousness of the issue, and proportionate to the behavior. Avoid insults, labels, threats, public spectacle, or assumptions about motive. More serious or repeated concerns may require a supervisor or formal procedure rather than peer confrontation. The chapter's 'pull up' practice is a brief correction, while deeper concerns are handled through a structured group process; that distinction helps prevent every issue from becoming a public trial.
Study instruction:
- Turn a personal accusation into a brief behavior-focused correction that states the expectation and invites a response.
Key point:
- The goal of correction is behavior change, not embarrassment.
- Clear boundaries and escalation routes protect the legitimacy of peer accountability.
43. Q: What is the difference between peer accountability and a blame culture?
A: Show answer
Peer accountability focuses on agreed standards, observable conduct, learning, correction, and follow-through. A blame culture focuses on assigning fault, protecting status, or punishing people for raising problems. In an accountable culture, people can disclose mistakes and receive proportionate feedback while still being responsible for correcting preventable behavior. In a blame culture, people hide information, shift responsibility, or retaliate against messengers. The chapter's examples show that social pressure can be used for good or ill; therefore, peer accountability must be designed to support the vital behavior and the purpose behind it, rather than simply increase social pressure.
Study instruction:
- Compare the likely response to an honest mistake in an accountable team and in a blame-oriented team.
Key point:
- Accountability makes expectations and corrective action explicit.
- Blame suppresses information and can reproduce the silence the change effort is trying to remove.
44. Q: How should an organization introduce a 200 percent accountability norm?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining a small number of observable vital behaviors and explaining why they matter. Leaders should model them and show how to respond when they are missed. Invite employees to discuss barriers and establish agreed language for brief peer corrections, information-sharing expectations, and routes for repeated or serious concerns. Practice through realistic scenarios before making the norm part of routine work. Supervisors should monitor whether feedback is accurate and respectful, and whether people who raise concerns are protected from retaliation. The norm should be introduced as mutual responsibility, not as permission for uncontrolled surveillance or personal criticism.
Study instruction:
- Build a staged implementation plan covering behavior definition, leadership modeling, practice, feedback channels, safeguards, and review.
Key point:
- A shared accountability norm needs clarity, modeling, practice, and safeguards.
- The organization must make speaking up safe as well as expected.
45. Q: When should peer accountability give way to formal escalation?
A: Show answer
Peer feedback is suitable for ordinary, low-risk deviations that can be corrected safely and respectfully. Serious safety threats, harassment, discrimination, fraud, safeguarding concerns, retaliation, repeated misconduct, or matters requiring investigation should be handled through the organization's formal reporting and escalation procedures. Employees should not be expected to confront a powerful person directly when doing so could expose them or others to danger. A peer-accountability system should clearly explain who receives information, how confidentiality is protected, and what happens after escalation. Informal dialogue can support a healthy culture, but it must never delay emergency action or replace mandatory reporting.
Study instruction:
- Classify several workplace concerns by whether they call for peer feedback, supervisor notification, or immediate formal escalation.
Key point:
- Peer correction is not a substitute for formal protections and investigations.
- Serious or unsafe matters require the appropriate reporting route.
46. Q: How can leaders tell whether a new social norm is taking hold?
A: Show answer
Look for observable behavior, not only positive statements in meetings. Evidence may include employees raising concerns earlier, peers recognizing desired actions, managers responding constructively, respected colleagues modeling the behavior, and teams discussing previously hidden issues. The chapter emphasizes that new norms take hold when people begin to defend them and that the speed of change depends on how quickly peers speak up when expectations are violated. Leaders should also examine whether the behavior is sustained across shifts, departments, and levels rather than concentrated among a small group of enthusiastic supporters.
Study instruction:
- Define three observable indicators that would demonstrate the norm in routine work, not merely at launch events.
Key point:
- A norm is established when it appears in everyday behavior and peer responses.
- Visible repetition across groups is stronger evidence than slogans or attendance records.
47. Q: What measures can be used to monitor a social-motivation change effort?
A: Show answer
A practical scorecard can combine leading indicators and outcome indicators. Leading indicators include the proportion of managers completing planned modeling and coaching actions, frequency of peer recognition, participation in structured feedback, and time between identifying a concern and raising it through the proper channel. Outcome indicators may include reduced recurrence of the targeted unsafe or ineffective behavior, improved service quality, fewer unresolved issues, or stronger employee reports of being able to speak up. Measures should be disaggregated by role and work location where appropriate. Increased reporting may indicate greater trust and visibility, not necessarily worsening underlying behavior, so interpret trends with context.
Study instruction:
- Build a small scorecard with at least two behavior indicators, two outcome indicators, and a note explaining each measure's limitations.
Key point:
- Measure actual behavior and results, not just awareness of the initiative.
- Interpret reporting trends carefully because better speaking up can initially increase recorded incidents.
48. Q: What common mistakes can undermine the use of social motivation?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include relying on slogans while leaders behave inconsistently; recruiting enthusiastic innovators instead of respected opinion leaders; asking peers to endorse a change without listening to their concerns; treating silence as agreement; and introducing peer correction without safeguards. Another failure is to praise only visible successes while ignoring the people who take risks to report problems. Leaders may also overuse social pressure, turning accountability into humiliation or conformity. These mistakes weaken credibility and can reproduce the very fear or silence the initiative is intended to remove. The corrective approach is to align leader behavior, engage credible messengers, make discussion safe, and define respectful accountability.
Study instruction:
- Review a change effort and identify which of these failure modes is most likely to occur first and how it would be detected.
Key point:
- Social pressure can reinforce harmful behavior as readily as constructive behavior.
- Credibility, inclusion, and safeguards are necessary to keep influence ethical and effective.
49. Q: How can leaders avoid confusing popularity with genuine opinion leadership?
A: Show answer
Popularity is not identical to influence, credibility, or suitability for a particular change. A person may be socially visible but not trusted on the issue, while a quieter employee may be highly respected within a professional or cultural subgroup. The chapter's nomination method asks who is most influential and respected, but leaders should interpret nominations in context and across groups. They should also assess whether a candidate models the vital behavior, communicates accurately, and is willing to engage constructively. Do not select people merely because they agree with management; doing so risks creating a chorus of endorsements rather than a credible partnership.
Study instruction:
- Test a proposed opinion leader against peer trust, network reach, topic credibility, behavioral example, and willingness to raise difficult feedback.
Key point:
- Influence is contextual and should not be reduced to visibility or popularity.
- A credible opinion leader must be trusted and able to model the desired behavior.
50. Q: How should leaders handle resistance from an opinion leader?
A: Show answer
Treat resistance as information to understand, not simply as disloyalty to overcome. Ask what the person believes is wrong, what practical or social risks they see, and what evidence would change their assessment. The chapter's examples show that leaders gained support by listening to candid feedback and making their own commitments visible. If the concern is valid, adjust the design or remove a barrier. If disagreement remains, explain the rationale honestly and avoid forcing a public endorsement. An opinion leader who feels manipulated may communicate skepticism more widely, while an honest discussion can reveal weaknesses before the initiative reaches the broader group.
Study instruction:
- Prepare open questions for a respected colleague who doubts a proposed change and identify how the plan could respond to legitimate concerns.
Key point:
- Resistance from a trusted peer can reveal design flaws and implementation risks.
- Do not demand endorsement before listening and addressing the substance of the objection.
51. Q: How do formal leaders, opinion leaders, stories, and peer accountability fit together?
A: Show answer
They perform complementary functions. Formal leaders provide authority, resources, direction, and visible modeling. Opinion leaders make the behavior credible within peer networks and help translate it into local practice. Stories and vicarious experiences help people see consequences and discuss issues that may otherwise remain hidden. Peer accountability sustains the behavior through daily encouragement and correction. If one element is missing, the change may stall: formal sponsorship without peer credibility can feel imposed; opinion leaders without leadership follow-through may lose trust; stories without action can create emotion without implementation; and accountability without safety can become coercive.
Study instruction:
- Map each of the four mechanisms to one specific action in a change initiative and identify the handoff between them.
Key point:
- Social change is strongest when leadership, peer credibility, shared stories, and accountability reinforce one another.
- No single communication event can substitute for an aligned social system.
52. Q: How can the chapter's social-motivation principles be applied to a safety improvement initiative?
A: Show answer
A safety initiative should begin with leaders visibly following the required practices and responding constructively when staff identify risks. Managers should enlist respected employees from relevant teams and shifts to model the behavior and explain practical barriers. Use verified incident stories or realistic scenarios to make the consequences discussable without exposing confidential details. Establish a clear peer process for immediate, respectful reminders, together with formal escalation for serious hazards or repeated violations. Monitor both safety behavior and willingness to report concerns. The aim is to make the safe action normal and speaking up an expected part of protecting colleagues and service users.
Study instruction:
- Design a safety intervention that includes one leadership sacrifice, an opinion-leader role, a story-based discussion, and a peer-accountability routine.
Key point:
- Safety norms depend on both individual practice and willingness to challenge unsafe behavior.
- Reporting and escalation arrangements must protect people and support timely corrective action.
53. Q: What ethical risks arise when leaders deliberately harness social pressure?
A: Show answer
Social influence can be used to encourage responsible behavior, but it can also produce conformity, intimidation, exclusion, or unquestioning obedience. The Milgram example warns that people may comply with authority or a peer even when they feel distress. Peer accountability can likewise become bullying if corrections are humiliating, biased, or disconnected from clear standards. Leaders should preserve the ability to disagree, protect people who raise concerns, distinguish evidence from rumor, and provide formal review for contested allegations. The objective is informed commitment to a justified behavior, not unquestioning loyalty to a leader or group.
Study instruction:
- Identify safeguards that would allow a team to challenge both the old norm and the change leader's proposal.
Key point:
- Influence should support responsible judgment, not suppress independent thought.
- Ethical safeguards include dignity, evidence, fair process, and protection from retaliation.
54. Q: How should leaders evaluate whether social influence is producing genuine rather than superficial compliance?
A: Show answer
Genuine change is reflected in behavior when the leader is absent, when the situation is inconvenient, and when peers face competing pressures. Superficial compliance may appear during formal events but disappear in routine work. Leaders should observe practice across different contexts, invite confidential feedback, examine whether employees can challenge authority, and test whether the behavior continues after initial attention fades. They should also check whether people understand the reason for the behavior rather than merely fearing disapproval. The chapter's emphasis on visible norms and peer defense suggests that sustained, distributed behavior is a stronger sign of change than a temporary burst of enthusiasm.
Study instruction:
- Choose a vital behavior and define how it would be observed during routine work, under pressure, and without direct supervision.
Key point:
- Durable norms persist beyond launch events and direct supervision.
- Understanding and voluntary commitment matter alongside visible conformity.
55. Q: What would you do if a respected peer publicly undermined a new vital behavior?
A: Show answer
First, determine whether the peer is raising a legitimate concern about the change or modeling behavior that contradicts an agreed standard. Speak with the person using specific examples and ask for the reasoning behind the objection. If the concern reveals a genuine flaw, bring it to the change team and address it openly. If the behavior is misinformation or a repeated violation, respond with accurate information and the same respectful accountability expected of everyone else. Avoid personal attacks or attempts to silence dissent. Because opinion leaders have broad influence, the quality of the response can either restore credibility or make the initiative appear defensive.
Study instruction:
- Work through a scenario in which an influential colleague criticizes a change and distinguish valid challenge from harmful conduct.
Key point:
- Do not equate disagreement with disloyalty.
- Address the substance openly and apply standards consistently.
56. Q: What is a practical sequence for applying the chapter's three lessons to a new change effort?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the vital behavior precisely and demonstrating it personally as a leader. Identify what sacrifice or visible commitment would make the request credible. Next, engage formal managers and map the informal network to identify respected opinion leaders across relevant groups. Listen to these people, adapt the plan where warranted, and give them a meaningful role in teaching and modeling the behavior. Then identify the unhealthy norm or silence that must change, make the issue discussable through evidence and stories, and establish respectful peer-accountability routines. Finally, monitor behavior, reporting safety, and outcomes, and revise the approach when feedback reveals barriers.
Study instruction:
- Create a one-page implementation sequence with actions, responsible roles, evidence, safeguards, and review points for each stage.
Key point:
- The chapter's sequence is leader modeling, formal and opinion-leader engagement, then peer accountability.
- Change becomes durable when the new behavior is credible, socially supported, and defended by peers.
Rows: 56
Table 6
Social Ability: Providing Assistance for Behavior Change
1. Q: What is social ability in the context of influencing behavior?
A: Show answer
Social ability is the support people receive from others that makes a desired behavior easier or more possible to perform. It is the fourth source of influence discussed in the chapter. Support may provide access to other people's knowledge, practical assistance, tools, authority, encouragement, or coaching. The central idea is that people rarely change through individual effort alone. Their behavior is enabled or constrained by the people around them, including colleagues, peers, managers, family members, and community members. A sound influence plan therefore examines not only whether people want to act differently, but also whether their social environment gives them the permission, examples, resources, and guidance required to do so.
Study instruction:
- Explain social ability in your own words and identify one behavior that depends on support from other people.
Key point:
- Social ability concerns the interpersonal conditions that make a behavior feasible.
- Leaders must remove social barriers as well as encourage individual effort.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
2. Q: Why does the chapter describe the people around us as social capital?
A: Show answer
Social capital refers to the human resources available through relationships and connections. Other people can contribute knowledge that an individual does not possess, practical effort that the individual cannot supply alone, access to resources, credibility, and guidance. The chapter uses Tanika's business group to show how modest contributions from several people can combine into a workable solution. Social capital is therefore more than having a large network. Its value depends on whether relationships provide useful assistance at the moment a person needs to perform a vital behavior. A network can enable progress, but it can also preserve restrictive norms or discourage change.
Study instruction:
- Map the people who could contribute knowledge, practical help, access, or credibility to a behavior-change effort.
Key point:
- Relationships can expand an individual's effective capability.
- The quality and usefulness of connections matter more than network size alone.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
3. Q: How is social ability different from social motivation?
A: Show answer
Social motivation concerns how other people influence a person's desire or willingness to act. Social ability concerns whether other people make the behavior easier to perform. The two are closely connected: offering help may communicate encouragement, while formal permission may make a previously discouraged action possible. However, the distinction remains useful for diagnosis. A person may want to report a project risk but lack permission to challenge a manager. Another person may be encouraged to use a new system but lack someone who can show how to complete a difficult task. The first problem requires enabling authority or norms; the second may require practical assistance or coaching.
Study instruction:
- Take one behavior and diagnose separately whether the person lacks willingness, social support, or both.
Key point:
- Social motivation addresses willingness; social ability addresses interpersonal enablement.
- Do not prescribe more encouragement when the actual barrier is lack of support or authority.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
4. Q: What four forms of social assistance should an influence plan provide?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies four forms: permission, modeling, help, and coaching. Permission signals that people may depart from old expectations and perform the new behavior. Modeling gives them a credible example of what the behavior looks like in practice. Help supplies tools, information, resources, or hands-on assistance when the person cannot yet act independently. Coaching provides feedback and guidance that improve the person's performance after an attempt. The forms address different barriers and are not interchangeable. A person may have permission but no skill, may understand the skill but lack a necessary tool, or may have a tool but remain uncertain about how to use it.
Study instruction:
- Create a four-column diagnostic for a behavior you want to change, recording the permission, model, help, and coaching required.
Key point:
- Permission, modeling, help, and coaching are distinct forms of social ability.
- Select support according to the actual barrier rather than relying on one intervention.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
5. Q: How should a leader diagnose whether a behavior requires more social ability?
A: Show answer
Start by observing the moment when the behavior should occur and asking what makes it difficult in the person's social environment. Determine who has authority over the behavior, what peers currently model, which people control needed information or resources, and who could coach a first attempt. Ask the person what would make the action easier and observe whether others reinforce or punish it. Look for unwritten rules, dependence on a specialist, missing tools, or situations in which the person is outnumbered or lacks influence. A useful diagnosis identifies a specific social barrier and connects it to a practical form of assistance.
Study instruction:
- Choose a vital behavior and interview people who perform, supervise, and observe it to identify social barriers at the point of action.
Key point:
- Diagnose the social barrier before selecting the intervention.
- Map authority, peer norms, practical resources, and coaching needs around the behavior.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
6. Q: What does Tanika's business-planning group demonstrate about social ability?
A: Show answer
Tanika's group demonstrates how collective knowledge can enable a person to do something that would be difficult alone. Her four peers challenged business ideas that required too much capital, faced excessive competition, or had weak earning potential. They helped her refine an idea for collecting and selling hair, offered hair themselves, suggested asking neighbors to contribute, and proposed using inexpensive toys as incentives for children who gathered hair. The group did not simply praise Tanika's ambition. It contributed local knowledge, tested assumptions, and helped construct a workable plan. The example shows that practical social assistance can turn a partially formed idea into executable behavior.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the group's contributions and distinguish encouragement from the concrete information and ideas that improved Tanika's plan.
Key point:
- Collective knowledge can improve the quality and feasibility of an individual's plan.
- Useful support tests assumptions and contributes practical solutions rather than offering praise alone.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
7. Q: Why was feedback from Tanika's peers more useful than simple encouragement?
A: Show answer
The group understood local purchasing power, investment constraints, competition, and the practical realities of village life. Its members could therefore identify why an egg business or a minivan rental required more capital than Tanika could initially access, and why entering a crowded puffed-rice market might be risky. They also knew what resources were available within the community. Their feedback was specific enough to eliminate weak options and generate a more viable alternative. Encouragement could have increased Tanika's confidence without improving her business decision. Social ability became valuable because the group supplied context-sensitive judgment that she could use immediately.
Study instruction:
- List the local assumptions that Tanika's peers tested and identify which of them would be relevant in another small-enterprise setting.
Key point:
- Useful peer feedback is grounded in relevant knowledge of the situation.
- Constructive challenge can be more enabling than unqualified approval.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
8. Q: How did Tanika's group turn individual ideas into a shared business solution?
A: Show answer
The group built on Tanika's partial idea through a sequence of contributions. Tanika identified a possible buyer for collected hair. Damini and Kamara offered hair from their own brushes and suggested extending collection to neighbors. Kamara proposed recruiting children, and Damini suggested offering inexpensive toys as an incentive. Each contribution addressed a different operational question: supply, collection reach, labor, and compensation. The final plan emerged through interaction rather than from a single expert. This illustrates a practical design principle: invite people with complementary knowledge to work on the same problem, and make their contributions specific enough to change the plan.
Study instruction:
- Break a current work problem into components and identify which people could contribute complementary knowledge to each component.
Key point:
- Shared problem-solving combines different contributions into a workable design.
- Structure collaboration around concrete questions and decisions, not an undirected discussion.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
9. Q: What role did group accountability play in the Grameen-style lending example?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that Tanika's group members were required to develop their own plans and cosign for one another's loans. This arrangement meant that each woman had a stake in the others' plans and repayment behavior. It encouraged members to scrutinize business ideas before the loans were granted and to support one another after starting. The example illustrates how social relationships can create both assistance and accountability. It should not be generalized into a universal lending rule: joint liability can also create pressure or unfair burdens if poorly designed. The relevant influence lesson is that shared commitments can make support consequential rather than symbolic.
Study instruction:
- Explain how peer review and shared commitments influenced the quality of the business plans, and identify possible risks of excessive peer pressure.
Key point:
- Shared responsibility can strengthen attention to planning and follow-through.
- Accountability mechanisms should support informed commitment without creating coercive or unfair pressure.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
10. Q: What does the chapter's group-eating example reveal about social influence?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports research in which people dining with one other person ate about 25 percent more than people eating alone, while those dining with four or more others ate over 60 percent more. It describes several possible social mechanisms: group meals last longer, food is passed around, people imitate others' choices, and shared bills can reduce the perceived cost of ordering more. The example illustrates that behavior often changes through ordinary social arrangements, even when people believe their decisions are purely personal. The reported percentages belong to the study as presented in the chapter; they should not be treated as universal effects for every meal or population.
Study instruction:
- Identify the social mechanisms described in the group-eating example and explain why the numerical findings should be interpreted in context.
Key point:
- Social settings can shape behavior without explicit persuasion.
- Routine interactions and group arrangements may enable or amplify behavior.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
11. Q: How can a team leader use social ability to encourage more candid discussion?
A: Show answer
A leader can influence candor by changing the social conditions of meetings. The chapter gives the example of a senior leader who waits to express an opinion until others have shared their views. This reduces the risk that the leader's position will anchor the discussion prematurely. A leader can also play devil's advocate and openly acknowledge weaknesses in their own proposal, demonstrating that disagreement is acceptable. These actions make it easier for others to contribute information that might otherwise remain unspoken. The leader should then respond constructively to dissent, ask questions, and ensure that speaking up does not lead to humiliation or retaliation.
Study instruction:
- Observe a meeting and assess whether the leader's timing, reactions, and treatment of dissent enable or suppress participation.
Key point:
- Leaders shape the social conditions in which people contribute ideas.
- Model openness to challenge before asking others to speak candidly.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
12. Q: Why can an individual possess the ability to perform a behavior but still be socially unable to do it?
A: Show answer
A person may have the knowledge and technical skill to act but face social constraints that make the action difficult or risky. A project manager may know how to report a delay but believe that admitting the problem will damage the team's reputation. A junior employee may understand a safety rule but feel unable to challenge a senior colleague. A clinician may know how to use a digital system but lack timely assistance when a difficult screen appears. In each case, individual capability exists, yet the surrounding relationships, norms, or resource arrangements block performance. Effective influence requires examining the social setting, not merely testing the individual's knowledge.
Study instruction:
- Find an example where someone knows what to do but is constrained by hierarchy, norms, or dependence on others.
Key point:
- Individual skill does not guarantee that the social environment permits action.
- Distinguish a personal skill gap from a social barrier before intervening.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
13. Q: What is social permission, and why is it sometimes necessary?
A: Show answer
Social permission is an explicit or implicit signal from relevant people that a new behavior is acceptable and supported. It is particularly important when the behavior conflicts with an established norm, hierarchy, or expectation. Employees may know that they should raise project risks but remain silent because managers have historically rewarded optimistic status reports. A community member may disapprove of abuse but feel unable to intervene because no safe response is recognized. Permission changes the social meaning of the behavior: it tells people that acting differently will not automatically result in disapproval or exclusion. It is most credible when those who previously enforced the old norm visibly support the new one.
Study instruction:
- Identify a behavior that conflicts with an unwritten rule and specify whose permission would make it safer to perform.
Key point:
- Permission removes a social constraint created by norms or authority.
- Signals must come from the people whose reactions influence whether the behavior feels acceptable.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
14. Q: How does formal permission differ from informal social permission?
A: Show answer
Informal permission may come from peers, respected colleagues, family members, or community members who signal that a behavior is accepted. Formal permission comes from people or institutions with recognized authority to change expectations, approve conduct, allocate resources, or protect those who act. The two may reinforce one another, but they are not identical. A peer may encourage an employee to report a risk, while a manager's explicit statement and subsequent response establish that reporting is genuinely expected. Where policies, legal duties, or safety requirements apply, informal encouragement cannot replace formal authorization or required procedures.
Study instruction:
- Map the formal and informal authorities that influence a behavior and identify where their messages might conflict.
Key point:
- Both informal and formal permission can affect behavior.
- Use formal authority when the barrier is embedded in organizational rules or hierarchy.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
15. Q: When should leaders explicitly authorize people to depart from an established social norm?
A: Show answer
Explicit authorization is useful when a desired behavior would violate an unwritten rule that has been reinforced by influential people. Examples include challenging unrealistic commitments, admitting a delay, questioning a senior person's proposal, or reporting a concern that others usually conceal. In these situations, general statements such as “be honest” may be insufficient because people have learned from experience that honesty carries a penalty. Leaders should identify the old expectation, state what behavior is now required, explain how it will be treated, and demonstrate that commitment through their own responses. Authorization must be consistent with law, policy, safety, and legitimate role boundaries.
Study instruction:
- Describe the old norm, the desired behavior, the authority needed to change the norm, and the observable signal that would confirm the change.
Key point:
- Norm-breaking behavior often requires explicit support from influential authorities.
- Permission is credible only when leaders' subsequent actions match their words.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
16. Q: What is project chicken, and how does it undermine project performance?
A: Show answer
Project chicken is the pattern described in the chapter in which project leaders claim that their work is on schedule even when they know they are behind. Each hopes that someone else will admit the need for more time and trigger a deadline extension. The first person to disclose the delay becomes the “chicken,” while everyone else benefits from the extension without making the admission. If nobody speaks up, the organization retains a false picture of readiness and may proceed toward an avoidable failure. The behavior damages planning, coordination, cost control, and trust because management decisions are based on misleading status information.
Study instruction:
- Draw the sequence of incentives in project chicken and identify the point at which truthful reporting would prevent downstream damage.
Key point:
- Project chicken is a collective norm of concealing schedule risk.
- False readiness reports prevent leaders from acting on accurate information.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
17. Q: Why did project chicken persist even though employees knew that false readiness reports were dangerous?
A: Show answer
The behavior was reinforced by the social environment, not merely by individual dishonesty. Managers, directors, and vice presidents had sent subtle signals that bad news was unwelcome, while employees feared being the first to admit a delay. The norm rewarded apparent confidence and shifted the immediate cost of disclosure onto the person who spoke first. Training and an anonymous survey did not by themselves change those expectations. Employees needed credible evidence that the very leaders whose disapproval they feared would accept and support early disclosure. The case demonstrates that entrenched behavior often survives information campaigns when the surrounding social consequences remain unchanged.
Study instruction:
- Separate individual incentives from organizational norms in a case where employees conceal bad news.
Key point:
- Repeated behavior may be sustained by leadership reactions and group expectations.
- Training alone cannot overcome a norm that still punishes the desired behavior.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
18. Q: How did Mike use formal leaders to change the project-chicken norm?
A: Show answer
