Mike, the new vice president of development, assigned senior leaders to teach communication classes that had previously been delivered by professional trainers. Every two weeks, a manager taught a two-hour session on speaking up about project risks. This placed leaders in a visible role of explaining and endorsing the new behavior. Employees could observe whether the managers who once discouraged candor were willing to teach it and support it in practice. The intervention addressed both the content of the training and the social authority behind it. It made the leaders part of the solution to a norm they had helped sustain.
Study instruction:
- Explain why changing the identity of the people teaching the classes was central to the intervention.
Key point:
- Who delivers a message can be as influential as the message itself.
- Enlist the authority figures who control the social consequences of the new behavior.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
19. Q: Why was it important that managers, rather than external trainers alone, taught the communication classes?
A: Show answer
Managers were the people whose reactions had previously made employees reluctant to speak honestly. When those same managers taught the classes, they publicly accepted responsibility for enabling candor. Their role communicated that the behavior was not merely a training slogan but an expectation supported by authority. It also gave employees an opportunity to observe whether managers could respond constructively when someone raised a real concern. External trainers could explain communication techniques, but they could not independently change the consequences employees expected from their own supervisors. The case shows why credible permission often requires visible participation by the people who control rewards, evaluations, and work priorities.
Study instruction:
- Compare what an external trainer can provide with what a direct manager must demonstrate for employees to feel permitted to speak up.
Key point:
- Formal leaders can make permission credible because they control important social consequences.
- Use training to reinforce a changed leadership behavior, not to substitute for it.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
20. Q: What happened when Jess first raised a project risk after receiving the new permission signal?
A: Show answer
Jess initially listened to the classes cynically, but by the third session he tested whether his manager's encouragement was genuine. He gently raised a risk to a customer commitment. His manager responded by acknowledging Jess's integrity and giving the information due consideration. That response mattered because it showed that raising a problem would not automatically result in punishment or dismissal. The change was reinforced through observable interaction, not simply through the words spoken in a classroom. By the sixth session, more peers had begun to speak up, and the new norm spread across the group.
Study instruction:
- Identify the first observable test of the new norm and explain how the manager's response affected later participation.
Key point:
- A first disclosure can test whether permission is genuine.
- Respond to early candor with attention, respect, and appropriate action.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
21. Q: How can a leader tell whether permission to speak up is genuine rather than symbolic?
A: Show answer
Genuine permission is demonstrated by consistent behavior after someone takes the risk of speaking. Leaders listen without ridicule, ask for relevant facts, acknowledge the contribution, consider the information, and explain what will happen next. They do not retaliate through assignments, performance reviews, exclusion, or informal punishment. They also correct systems that continue to reward concealment. A policy statement or training session is weak evidence if employees still observe negative consequences for raising concerns. Leaders should monitor both formal outcomes and informal reactions, because employees often judge permission by what happens to the first few people who use it.
Study instruction:
- Design a short review that tracks leader responses to the first several instances of employees raising concerns.
Key point:
- Permission is validated by the consequences people experience after acting.
- Monitor retaliation, dismissive reactions, and follow-through as well as written policy.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
22. Q: What is modeling as a form of social ability?
A: Show answer
Modeling means showing people what a desired behavior looks like through a credible example. A model makes an abstract instruction concrete by demonstrating the words, actions, judgment, and practical choices involved. People often need to see someone they regard as similar or credible performing the behavior in a real situation. The chapter's example of Kade shows how observing a peer admit a serious lie influenced Kade more powerfully than general reminders about honesty. Modeling reduces uncertainty about how to act and can make a new behavior appear socially possible. It is especially valuable when the behavior is unfamiliar, emotionally difficult, or contrary to habit.
Study instruction:
- Identify a behavior that is difficult to explain verbally and describe what a credible live model would demonstrate.
Key point:
- Modeling turns an abstract expectation into observable behavior.
- Choose models whose conduct and credibility make the example persuasive to the intended audience.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
23. Q: Why are credible and relatable models often more influential than distant experts?
A: Show answer
People are more likely to learn from a model when they can recognize the situation, constraints, and pressures involved. A respected peer may demonstrate that the behavior is achievable by someone with similar responsibilities and social standing. An expert may possess greater technical knowledge but may be perceived as too distant from the learner's daily reality. Credibility also depends on whether the model's behavior is consistent with the stated standard. The chapter emphasizes opinion leaders because they already have natural influence within their groups. They can make a new practice visible in the places where people actually work and interact.
Study instruction:
- Compare two possible models for a new behavior and assess similarity, credibility, access, and demonstrated competence.
Key point:
- Relatability helps people see a new behavior as attainable.
- Select models for trust and practical relevance, not title or seniority alone.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
24. Q: How did Kade's peer model influence his decision to admit his own lie?
A: Show answer
Kade had concealed important information during his initial interview at The Other Side Academy, despite the community's strong emphasis on truthfulness. Six months later, he saw a close peer wearing a yellow shirt, which indicated that the peer was on contract after admitting a lie in his own interview. The peer explained that he had confessed because he wanted to live differently and regain trust. Observing someone close to him accept consequences for honesty made the choice concrete. Kade then disclosed his own lie and accepted a month of extra work. The example shows how a credible peer can make a difficult behavior seem possible and worthwhile.
Study instruction:
- Identify why the peer's example affected Kade and distinguish the effect of the model from the community's formal disciplinary process.
Key point:
- A peer model can make integrity visible through action and accepted consequences.
- Demonstration is especially powerful when the learner trusts the person being observed.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
25. Q: What is the role of opinion leaders in behavior modeling?
A: Show answer
Opinion leaders are people who have natural influence within a group, often because others trust their judgment or seek their advice. In the chapter, they are valuable models because their behavior is visible and credible to the people leaders want to influence. They can demonstrate new practices in ordinary interactions, answer questions in context, and help a behavior spread through existing relationships. Identifying opinion leaders requires understanding whom people actually turn to, not merely selecting those with formal titles. Their involvement should be supported with clear expectations and preparation so that they model the desired behavior accurately and consistently.
Study instruction:
- Identify opinion leaders by asking members of a group whose advice they seek and whose behavior they observe.
Key point:
- Opinion leaders connect formal change efforts with everyday social influence.
- Choose models based on actual peer credibility rather than organizational rank alone.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
26. Q: How did Carol Bernick use growth development leaders at Alberto Culver?
A: Show answer
Carol Bernick concluded that the company's turnaround required broad employee engagement rather than a narrow focus on new products. She identified 70 opinion leaders across the enterprise, spent months listening to them and earning their trust, and invited willing participants to become growth development leaders. These leaders were asked to enable greater engagement among colleagues who were naturally influenced by them. They could also bring a colleague to sessions with Bernick, where the colleague observed candid exchanges between the president and the growth development leaders. Those sessions modeled how to participate openly with senior leadership and gave employees a pattern they could take back to their teams.
Study instruction:
- Trace the sequence from identifying opinion leaders to creating visible models of candid participation across the company.
Key point:
- Opinion leaders can help spread new interaction patterns through trusted relationships.
- Expose colleagues to the behavior in action, not only to descriptions of the desired culture.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
27. Q: How did Hope Squad use peer modeling to support student suicide prevention?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Hope Squad as a peer-to-peer program that trains students identified by classmates as people they would approach for help. Its central practice is QPR: Question, Persuade, and Refer. Student members learn to notice concerns, open a conversation, encourage a student at risk to seek help, and connect that person with appropriate resources. The model recognizes that peers may notice distress before adults do. Its influence mechanism is social credibility: students may be more willing to respond to a trusted peer. The program's peer role is to connect and refer, not to replace qualified mental-health professionals or emergency services.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the QPR sequence and distinguish peer recognition and referral from professional assessment or treatment.
Key point:
- Peer models can extend the reach of support and help connect people with resources.
- Train peer supporters clearly and preserve appropriate adult and professional escalation.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
28. Q: What safeguards are needed when peer models are used in sensitive or high-risk situations?
A: Show answer
Peer models should be trained for a defined role, supported by responsible adults or professionals, and given clear procedures for escalating situations beyond their competence. They should not be expected to investigate serious allegations, provide clinical treatment, guarantee confidentiality where safety is at risk, or confront dangerous people alone. The chapter's Hope Squad example emphasizes connecting a student with help; its wider lesson is that peer credibility can improve access to support. In sensitive contexts, leaders must also protect the person seeking help and the peer supporter, maintain appropriate privacy, and follow safeguarding, emergency, and reporting requirements.
Study instruction:
- Review a peer-support proposal and list the situations that must be referred to trained professionals or formal safeguarding channels.
Key point:
- Peer support is an access bridge, not a substitute for professional responsibility.
- Define boundaries, supervision, confidentiality limits, and escalation before launching a peer model.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
29. Q: What does providing help mean as a form of social ability?
A: Show answer
Providing help means supplying the practical assistance people need to perform a behavior. That assistance may include a tool, information, a resource, access to another person, or hands-on support. It differs from permission because it addresses whether the person can act, not simply whether the person is allowed to act. It differs from modeling because seeing an example may not remove a practical obstacle. It differs from coaching because immediate help may solve a task problem without developing the person's skill over time. Effective leaders identify the moment of difficulty and provide assistance that is specific enough to let the person take the next step.
Study instruction:
- Describe the practical obstacle in a behavior and identify the smallest useful form of assistance that would remove it.
Key point:
- Help removes a practical barrier to performing the behavior.
- Provide the resource or assistance needed at the point where the person is stuck.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
30. Q: What forms can practical help take?
A: Show answer
Practical help can include equipment, a template, access rights, information, time, a knowledgeable colleague, a backup person, or direct assistance with a task. The appropriate form depends on the barrier. A person learning a digital system may need someone nearby to troubleshoot a common problem. A team facing knowledge silos may need paired work so that expertise is shared. A person attempting to intervene safely may need a recognized tool or procedure rather than another lecture. The chapter's examples show that useful help is often designed around predictable moments of difficulty, not delivered as a generic resource package detached from the work.
Study instruction:
- List the common points of failure in a task and match each one to a concrete resource or assistance mechanism.
Key point:
- Help can be material, informational, relational, or hands-on.
- Design support around the actual task and the moment assistance is required.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
31. Q: How did Soul City's pot-and-pan example illustrate the value of a practical tool?
A: Show answer
Garth Japhet recognized that people who disapproved of domestic violence sometimes felt unable to intervene safely or effectively. In the television story he developed, neighbors who heard abuse gathered outside the home and banged pots and pans to signal that the violence was not unnoticed. The chapter reports that people in townships began using the action after seeing the program. The example illustrates how a simple, recognizable tool can turn a vague desire to help into a specific behavior people can perform together. Its lesson is about enabling action, not about prescribing this tactic for every real-world abuse situation.
Study instruction:
- Explain what capability gap the pot-and-pan action addressed and identify why a real-world intervention still requires safety planning.
Key point:
- A simple tool can make an otherwise uncertain action more concrete.
- Practical interventions must be adapted to context and must not expose people to avoidable danger.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
32. Q: Why did Japhet offer a tool instead of relying on more lectures about violence?
A: Show answer
Many people already disapproved of violence, so additional persuasion was unlikely to solve the main barrier. They lacked a safe, practical way to respond when they witnessed abuse. Japhet therefore focused on enabling behavior rather than repeating reasons to oppose the problem. The tool provided a shared action that neighbors could recognize and coordinate. This illustrates a general diagnostic rule: when people already accept the goal but do not act, investigate ability barriers before increasing motivational messages. In contemporary practice, any intervention involving abuse must also be designed with survivor safety, local services, legal requirements, and the risk of escalation in mind.
Study instruction:
- Identify a situation in which people agree that action is needed but lack a safe or practical method of acting.
Key point:
- When motivation already exists, capability support may be the missing intervention.
- Do not assume more persuasion will solve a problem caused by lack of tools or safe procedures.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
33. Q: How did paired programming at Menlo Software provide practical help?
A: Show answer
Rich Sheridan arranged for software developers to work in pairs, sharing one computer. One person typed while the other co-designed and watched, so each task had more than one person actively considering the work. The arrangement initially felt restrictive to people who preferred individual control, but the teams began catching errors earlier and reducing dependence on a single person's knowledge. The chapter reports that time spent fixing errors fell from 40 percent of working time to no time at all in the described setting. It also reduced knowledge silos and made vacations easier to accommodate because more than one person understood each job.
Study instruction:
- Explain how paired work changed error detection, knowledge distribution, and operational continuity in the example.
Key point:
- Interdependent work can provide immediate assistance while spreading knowledge.
- Design collaboration so that help is built into the workflow rather than requested only after failure.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
34. Q: What are knowledge silos, and why can they make an organization fragile?
A: Show answer
A knowledge silo exists when important information or expertise is concentrated in one person or isolated group. Work may stop when that person is absent, overloaded, or leaves the organization. Others may be unable to review decisions, detect mistakes, or provide backup because they do not understand the task. The Menlo example shows how pairing people can distribute knowledge and create shared ownership. Reducing silos does not mean that everyone must know everything. It means identifying critical dependencies and ensuring that essential work has adequate coverage, documentation, and opportunities for others to learn.
Study instruction:
- Identify critical tasks that depend on one individual and propose a practical way to distribute knowledge and backup capacity.
Key point:
- Concentrated knowledge creates continuity and control risks.
- Use pairing, documentation, cross-training, and backup arrangements for critical work.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
35. Q: How did Spectrum Healthcare's Purple People support adoption of a new electronic medical record system?
A: Show answer
Before the system went live, leaders anticipated that busy doctors and nurses might avoid the new process and continue using sticky notes or scratch pads. They trained opinion leaders from across the organization to help colleagues with common problems. During the first week, these helpers wore purple shirts marked Purple People, making them easy to identify. Staff and doctors were told whom to approach when they encountered difficulty. The chapter reports 98 percent compliance within 48 hours. The intervention paired a clear expectation with visible, accessible assistance at the moment users needed it.
Study instruction:
- Identify the adoption risk, the support role, and the design features that made Purple People easy to find during implementation.
Key point:
- Visible, accessible help can reduce friction during a major process change.
- Make support recognizable and available where the new behavior is performed.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
36. Q: What design principles can be drawn from the Purple People example?
A: Show answer
First, anticipate the predictable problems users will encounter rather than waiting for complaints. Second, recruit helpers who are trusted or knowledgeable within the relevant work groups. Third, make those helpers easy to identify at the point of use. Fourth, tell users exactly where to go when they become stuck. Fifth, provide assistance during the critical early period when habits are forming. The reported compliance result belongs to the chapter's case and should not be treated as a guaranteed outcome for every technology rollout. The transferable principle is to make support visible, timely, and practical.
Study instruction:
- Develop a first-week support plan for a new system, including helper selection, visibility, escalation, and coverage times.
Key point:
- Adoption support should be easy to locate at the moment of need.
- Plan for predictable user difficulties before implementation begins.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
37. Q: Why is asking for help not a sign of weakness?
A: Show answer
People often relapse into familiar behavior because new habits require effort, skill, and support. The chapter argues that arranging help is an acknowledgment of human interdependence, not a personal failure. A person may need a colleague to check work, explain a process, provide backup, or help manage a difficult moment. Leaders who normalize assistance make it easier for people to persist through early mistakes instead of hiding them. This also improves organizational resilience because support is not concentrated in isolated experts. Help should build capability and independence where possible, rather than create unnecessary dependence on a permanent rescuer.
Study instruction:
- Identify one new behavior that would become easier if help were treated as a normal part of learning rather than as an exception.
Key point:
- Seeking assistance can be a rational way to sustain behavior change.
- Normalize help-seeking while building the person's capacity to act independently.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
38. Q: What is coaching, and what problem does it solve?
A: Show answer
Coaching is targeted guidance that helps a person see their behavior more accurately and improve how they perform it. The chapter explains that people may know what they want to achieve but lack an accurate picture of how their actions affect others. A coach observes behavior, identifies a specific gap, and offers actionable guidance that the person can apply. Coaching therefore goes beyond encouragement and may go beyond supplying a tool. It helps the learner refine technique through feedback and practice. It is particularly useful when a person has begun attempting the desired behavior but needs to improve quality, timing, judgment, or effectiveness.
Study instruction:
- Identify a behavior that someone is already attempting and specify what an observer would need to notice before giving useful coaching.
Key point:
- Coaching improves the quality of performance through observation and feedback.
- Give specific guidance based on observed behavior rather than general judgments.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
39. Q: How did coaching help Lauren become a more effective speaker?
A: Show answer
Lauren was told that she needed to participate more at work. She increased her contributions and volunteered to present ideas, but her credibility fell because her presentations were slow and poorly organized. A colleague observed her in multiple settings and gave two precise suggestions: increase her speaking speed by about 10 percent and begin with her conclusion. Lauren found the feedback difficult to hear, but she applied it and noticed greater audience attention. Her confidence and influence improved. The case shows that motivation to participate was not the missing factor; she needed an accurate diagnosis and practical coaching on how to perform.
Study instruction:
- Practice turning a vague development goal such as “be more influential” into observable behaviors and specific feedback.
Key point:
- Lauren needed skill coaching, not additional pressure to participate.
- Useful feedback identifies a behavior the learner can change immediately.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
40. Q: What makes coaching different from encouragement, training, mentoring, and practical help?
A: Show answer
Encouragement communicates that a person should continue or attempt a behavior. Training usually develops knowledge or skills for a group or in a structured setting. Mentoring may provide broader career guidance and perspective over time. Practical help supplies a tool, resource, or immediate assistance. Coaching focuses on the individual's observed performance and gives feedback intended to improve the next attempt. These forms can complement one another. For example, a person may receive permission to speak candidly, attend training on risk communication, use a reporting template, and then receive coaching after raising a concern. The key is to identify which need remains unmet.
Study instruction:
- Compare the five forms of support in a real development situation and explain which one should be used first.
Key point:
- Different forms of support solve different problems.
- Do not confuse encouragement or information with performance-specific coaching.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
41. Q: Why should coaching be connected closely to a person's attempt at a new behavior?
A: Show answer
Feedback is easier to apply when the person can connect it to a recent action and still remembers the situation. Real-time or prompt coaching helps identify what worked, what did not, and what to change on the next attempt. It also prevents vague impressions from accumulating into delayed performance reviews that offer little guidance for immediate improvement. The chapter's summary emphasizes that learning accelerates when coaching follows an initial attempt. Coaching should remain respectful and specific, and it should focus on behaviors within the learner's control rather than attacking personality or assigning motives.
Study instruction:
- Design a short coaching conversation to take place immediately after a real attempt, using one observed strength and one actionable adjustment.
Key point:
- Timely feedback links guidance to a concrete experience.
- Coach close to the behavior while the details are still clear.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
42. Q: How did KIPP's Near Peers use social connections to support college persistence?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes KIPP replacing expert school counselors for this purpose with Near Peers: former KIPP students who had recently graduated from college. They could work for up to two years helping younger KIPP alumni navigate early college life. Because they had recently faced similar challenges, they could offer practical advice about course choices, relationships, and managing limited money. Their credibility came partly from shared experience and proximity to the students' circumstances. The chapter reports that 45 percent of KIPP alumni who graduated from high school were completing a four-year degree after the shift. The example illustrates how relevant peer experience can complement formal expertise.
Study instruction:
- Explain why recent graduates could provide advice that students might find especially credible and identify what professional support they still may need.
Key point:
- Near Peers combine relevant lived experience with an accessible support relationship.
- Use peer guidance to complement, not automatically replace, specialist services.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
43. Q: What does the Thailand coaching example teach about enabling behavior under pressure?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Dr. Wiwat Rojanapithayakorn's response to the AIDS epidemic in Thailand in the early 1980s. The intervention focused on moments in which sex workers faced pressure to agree to unprotected sex. Wiwat worked with brothel owners to establish a condom-use requirement and a monitoring consequence, then involved former sex workers and more experienced workers in training and coaching younger women. The plan addressed authority, practical skill, peer backup, and pressure at the point of decision. The chapter reports that condom use rose from 14 percent to 90 percent and new infections later fell substantially. The case illustrates an integrated social intervention, not a current medical protocol.
Study instruction:
- Map the authority, peer coaching, rehearsal, and backup elements in the historical case, while distinguishing them from present-day health guidance.
Key point:
- Behavior under pressure requires more than information or personal resolve.
- Combine authority, skill practice, and real-time social backup at critical moments.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
44. Q: Why did Wiwat involve brothel owners as well as sex workers and peer coaches?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the owners as people who controlled the working environment and could either enable or undermine the desired behavior. Wiwat sought their commitment to a uniform condom-use requirement and introduced monitoring through mystery shoppers and a possible 30-day closure. This gave workers greater authority to refuse unprotected services and reduced the risk that one establishment would lose business by insisting on protection while competitors did not. The intervention also recognized that workers needed practical coaching and backup in difficult encounters. The case shows that a behavior plan must include people who control the conditions under which the behavior occurs.
Study instruction:
- Identify who controls the rules, resources, and immediate social consequences in a workplace where a frontline worker must maintain a safety boundary.
Key point:
- People with structural authority must be included when they control the conditions for behavior.
- Support frontline behavior by changing both local capability and the environment's rules.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
45. Q: How did deliberate practice prepare workers for difficult moments in the Thailand example?
A: Show answer
Former sex workers led workshops and helped identify more experienced workers who could coach colleagues. Participants practiced how to respond to pressure from customers with the very coworkers who would be present when the situation became difficult. They also knew whom to turn to for backup. This made the learning relevant to the actual social setting rather than an abstract classroom exercise. Deliberate practice can expose uncertainty, allow feedback, and build a rehearsed response before pressure occurs. The chapter's wider lesson is that coaching should prepare people for the precise moments when the vital behavior is most likely to be challenged.
Study instruction:
- Create a rehearsal scenario for a high-pressure behavior and specify who will observe, coach, and provide backup.
Key point:
- Practice should resemble the real situation in which the behavior must occur.
- Rehearse critical responses with the people who will be present when pressure arises.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
46. Q: What is the network quotient, and how does it relate to social ability?
A: Show answer
The chapter attributes the expression network quotient, or NQ, to Tom Boyle of British Telecom. It is used to emphasize a person's ability to form connections with others. The chapter presents the argument that, from a career perspective, this capacity may be more important than intelligence quotient alone. In the context of social ability, the point is that useful relationships provide access to advice, mentoring, resources, opportunities, and support. The concept should be understood as the chapter's framing rather than as a universal measurement rule. Network value depends on the quality, reciprocity, relevance, and responsible use of connections.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a person's relationships can provide access to knowledge, opportunity, and support, and identify limitations of treating networks as a single score.
Key point:
- Connections can extend an individual's capacity and access to resources.
- Build useful, reciprocal relationships rather than focusing only on the size of a network.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
47. Q: How can social connections enable or inhibit patterns of poverty and prosperity?
A: Show answer
Social connections affect access to information, mentoring, guidance, capital, opportunities, and practical assistance. The chapter's examples show how a supportive group can help someone test a business idea, while the absence of access to capital and advice can trap people in poor options. The same principle applies to career and education: people with knowledgeable contacts may learn how to navigate systems that are unfamiliar to them. Connections can also inhibit change when a network enforces harmful norms, discourages disclosure, or withholds support. Leaders should therefore assess not just whether a network exists, but what behavior it reinforces and who is excluded from its resources.
Study instruction:
- Map the enabling and constraining effects of a network in a community, team, or career pathway.
Key point:
- Networks can distribute opportunity or reproduce barriers.
- Strengthen supportive connections while identifying relationships that reinforce harmful norms.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
48. Q: How should leaders combine permission, modeling, help, and coaching in one influence plan?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the vital behavior and the moment when it must occur. Determine whether people need authority to depart from old norms, a credible example to show what the behavior looks like, practical resources to make it possible, or feedback to improve performance. Then combine the relevant forms. For example, an employee asked to report risks may need a manager to authorize candor, a respected peer to demonstrate a good report, a simple reporting template, and coaching after the first disclosure. The forms reinforce one another: permission lowers social risk, modeling reduces uncertainty, help removes friction, and coaching develops competence.
Study instruction:
- Build an integrated support plan for one behavior and assign an observable intervention to each of the four forms.
Key point:
- An effective social-ability plan is matched to the complete set of barriers.
- Use the four forms together when the behavior requires authority, example, resources, and skill refinement.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
49. Q: How can an influence leader map the people who need to participate in a change effort?
A: Show answer
Map the people who perform the behavior, those who supervise or authorize it, the peers whose approval matters, the individuals who possess relevant expertise, and the people who can provide practical backup. Include informal opinion leaders as well as formal managers. Ask who is consulted when a problem arises, whose reaction can make a behavior risky, and who can demonstrate the desired practice credibly. The map should also identify people who may be affected by the change but are not represented in decision-making. This analysis reveals where permission, modeling, help, and coaching must come from and where conflicting social signals may undermine the effort.
Study instruction:
- Create a stakeholder map that distinguishes formal authority, informal influence, expertise, frontline participation, and affected groups.
Key point:
- Behavior is shaped by a network of relationships, not by a single leader.
- Include the people who enable, constrain, model, and experience the behavior.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
50. Q: What indicators can be used to evaluate whether social ability is improving?
A: Show answer
Use indicators that correspond to the form of support being provided. For permission, examine whether people raise concerns, challenge assumptions, and receive constructive responses without retaliation. For modeling, observe whether credible peers demonstrate the behavior in routine settings and whether others begin to imitate it. For help, track whether required tools and assistance are available at the point of need and how quickly users receive support. For coaching, assess the specificity and timeliness of feedback and whether performance improves across attempts. Combine these measures with outcomes, but do not assume that a single result proves which intervention caused the change.
Study instruction:
- Design a small scorecard with at least one observable indicator for each of the four forms of social ability.
Key point:
- Measure the social support process as well as the final behavior and outcome.
- Use multiple indicators and interpret changes in context.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
51. Q: What common mistakes weaken attempts to provide social ability?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include giving motivational speeches when people lack tools, delivering training while managers continue to punish the desired behavior, selecting models whom the audience does not trust, making helpers difficult to find, and offering coaching that is vague or delayed. Another mistake is assuming that a written policy automatically creates permission. Leaders may also ask peer supporters to handle situations beyond their competence or fail to provide backup. These errors occur when interventions are selected for convenience rather than from a diagnosis of the actual barrier. A strong plan tests whether people can perform the behavior under real conditions and adjusts support when evidence shows that they cannot.
Study instruction:
- Review a change initiative and identify at least three points where the intervention may not match the actual social barrier.
Key point:
- Support fails when it does not address the conditions that prevent action.
- Match each intervention to a diagnosed barrier and check whether the support is usable.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
52. Q: What should a manager do if employees continue to hide project delays after receiving communication training?
A: Show answer
The manager should investigate the social consequences of truthful reporting rather than simply repeat the training. Ask employees what happens when someone reports bad news, examine whether deadlines and performance measures reward unrealistic optimism, and identify managers whose reactions discourage disclosure. Senior leaders should explicitly authorize early risk reporting, demonstrate how to discuss uncertainty, and respond constructively when the first employee raises a concern. The organization may also need a clear process for updating forecasts and escalating schedule risks. The objective is to make accurate reporting safer and more useful than concealment, while preserving accountability for realistic planning.
Study instruction:
- Apply the project-chicken diagnosis to a current project and specify the leadership response, reporting process, and first test of changed behavior.
Key point:
- Persistent concealment usually requires changing the social consequences of disclosure.
- Training must be reinforced by leader behavior and a credible risk-reporting process.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
53. Q: How should an organization support employees during a major technology rollout?
A: Show answer
Identify the tasks users must perform, the points where they are likely to become stuck, and the people who can provide credible assistance. Recruit and train accessible helpers from relevant work groups, make them easy to recognize, and publish clear instructions for obtaining support. Provide practice before launch where possible, ensure that common questions have reliable answers, and establish escalation for problems that helpers cannot resolve. Monitor actual use and the reasons for workarounds rather than treating noncompliance as automatically intentional. The Purple People example shows the value of visible support during the early adoption period, but the support plan should be adapted to the system and users.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a rollout support map covering user groups, common difficulties, named helpers, escalation, and early adoption measures.
Key point:
- Technology adoption depends on accessible social support as well as system availability.
- Put trained help at the point of use during the period when new habits are forming.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
54. Q: What would help a junior employee challenge an unsafe or unrealistic instruction from a senior colleague?
A: Show answer
The employee may need explicit permission from management to raise safety concerns, a credible model of respectful challenge, a clear reporting or stop-work procedure, and access to someone who can provide immediate backup. The leader should make the expected behavior clear and demonstrate that concerns will be considered on their merits. If there is immediate danger, the employee should follow applicable emergency and safety procedures rather than waiting for a routine conversation. Social ability is not a substitute for formal protections, legal duties, or incident reporting. It should make correct action more feasible within those systems.
Study instruction:
- Map the authority and support required for a junior employee to raise a safety concern without being left to manage the risk alone.
Key point:
- Power differences can make correct behavior socially difficult.
- Pair permission and peer support with formal safety procedures and escalation routes.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
55. Q: How can an organization sustain social ability after an initial change campaign ends?
A: Show answer
Make support part of ordinary work rather than a temporary campaign. Leaders should continue to demonstrate permission through routine decisions, opinion leaders should keep modeling the behavior, practical help should remain accessible, and coaching should be integrated into regular feedback and supervision. New employees need to learn the norms and know where to obtain assistance. Organizations should monitor whether old patterns return when senior attention shifts elsewhere. Succession planning matters because a system that depends on one enthusiastic champion may collapse when that person leaves. Sustained social ability is reflected in relationships, routines, and structures that continue to enable the behavior.
Study instruction:
- Identify which supports in a current change effort would disappear if its project team or champion left tomorrow.
Key point:
- Social support must become part of the operating environment to endure.
- Embed permission, models, help, and coaching in routine management and onboarding.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
56. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 6 about making behavior change possible?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that people often need more than reasons to change. They need a social environment that enables the new behavior. Effective leaders identify who must grant permission, who can model the behavior credibly, what practical help is required, and who can coach people as they improve. The examples range from Tanika's peer group and project-risk reporting to peer support, technology adoption, and public-health interventions. Across these settings, the same principle applies: behavior is socially embedded. Change becomes more achievable when leaders deliberately build the relationships, authority, resources, and feedback that allow people to act differently.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter using one practical example for each of the four forms of social ability.
Key point:
- Behavior change depends on the social conditions surrounding the individual.
- Make the desired behavior permitted, visible, feasible, and progressively more skillful.
Source links:
- Primary source: Chapter_06.txt, “Provide Assistance” (supplied ; no public URL supplied).
Rows: 56
Table 7
Structural Motivation: Rewards, Incentives, and Discipline
1. Q: What is structural motivation, or source 5, in the chapter's influence framework?
A: Show answer
Structural motivation is the influence created by the external consequences attached to behavior: rewards, perks, bonuses, salaries, privileges, sanctions, and other economic or material arrangements. It asks whether the surrounding system makes a vital behavior more or less attractive to perform. The chapter treats this as one of six sources of influence, but distinguishes it from personal motivation and social motivation. Structural motivation can reinforce a desired action, yet it can also unintentionally reward the very behavior leaders are trying to change. Its value therefore depends less on the size of an incentive than on whether the incentive supports the right behavior for the right reasons.
Study instruction:
- Define source 5 in your own words and classify five examples of workplace rewards, privileges, or sanctions as structural motivators.
Key point:
- Structural motivation changes the consequences attached to behavior.
- An incentive can support or undermine the intended influence strategy.
2. Q: What is the primary objective of structural motivation when leaders seek behavior change?
A: Show answer
The chapter's first recommendation is to remove disincentives rather than overwhelm people with large rewards. A system may make the undesired behavior easier, faster, more profitable, more visible, or safer for the individual than the desired behavior. In that situation, adding a bonus may leave the underlying economic arrangement intact. Leaders should examine what the current system pays for, tolerates, measures, and makes convenient, then remove features that contradict the desired behavior. Structural motivation is most useful when it aligns the environment with the change already being encouraged through personal and social influence.
Study instruction:
- Choose one unwanted behavior and map the pay, workload, recognition, convenience, and penalty arrangements that may be sustaining it.
Key point:
- First remove structural barriers and rewards for the wrong behavior.
- Alignment is more important than the absolute size of an incentive.
3. Q: Why does the chapter advise leaders to use extrinsic rewards third?
A: Show answer
The recommended sequence is to establish personal motivation first, social motivation second, and structural motivation third. People should understand why the vital behavior is right, meaningful, or worthwhile, and influential peers and leaders should help make it a social norm. Only after those foundations are addressed should leaders adjust rewards or sanctions. Starting with money can recast a moral or cooperative responsibility as a transaction, invite bargaining, or weaken existing intrinsic reasons to act. Using rewards third does not mean ignoring incentives; it means preventing them from carrying the entire motivational burden.
Study instruction:
- Write a short change plan that addresses personal meaning first, social support second, and structural consequences third.
Key point:
- Personal and social motivation should do the heavy lifting.
- Extrinsic incentives should reinforce, not replace, the other sources.
4. Q: How do personal, social, and structural motivation work together?
A: Show answer
Personal motivation concerns the individual's reasons, values, and satisfaction; social motivation concerns the expectations and support of important others; structural motivation concerns external rewards and costs. The chapter argues that lasting change usually requires all six sources of influence, not a single reward mechanism. A small token may become meaningful because it recognizes personal effort and signals respect from a group. Conversely, a large bonus can be counterproductive if it makes a previously meaningful activity feel like paid labor or encourages competition where cooperation is needed. Effective design asks how the sources reinforce one another rather than treating them as separate levers.
Study instruction:
- For a change initiative, identify one personal, one social, and one structural factor that could reinforce the same vital behavior.
Key point:
- The sources are complementary rather than interchangeable.
- A reward can carry symbolic and social meaning beyond its cash value.
5. Q: How can a poorly designed incentive system increase the behavior it was intended to reduce?
A: Show answer
People respond to the behavior that the reward actually measures or makes advantageous, not necessarily to the outcome leaders have in mind. If a metric is incomplete, individuals may optimize the rewarded quantity while sacrificing quality, teamwork, safety, or long-term value. A production bonus based only on volume, for example, may encourage shortcuts or low-value output. The chapter's cases show that incentives can redirect attention, change the meaning of an activity, and create new negotiations or workarounds. The first diagnostic question should therefore be: what behavior would a rational person maximize under this reward arrangement?
Study instruction:
- Take one organizational target and identify at least two ways a person could meet its metric while damaging the intended outcome.
Key point:
- People adapt to the actual incentive, not the slogan behind it.
- Unbalanced metrics invite gaming and unintended trade-offs.
6. Q: What does the anesthesiologist example teach about individual performance pay and teamwork?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a hospital where anesthesiologists were paid according to work performed for their own patients. The arrangement reduced their willingness to assist when another clinician's patient experienced a serious reaction. The reward system made individual production financially visible while treating help for a colleague as uncompensated work. This illustrates a conflict between individual incentives and collective outcomes: a hospital needs clinicians to respond to patient needs across team boundaries, not merely maximize their own caseload. A better design would recognize shared responsibilities and protect time for assistance rather than rewarding only individually attributable activity.
Study instruction:
- Review a team-based service and list tasks that are essential to collective success but invisible in individual productivity measures.
Key point:
- Individual pay can discourage cooperation when help is not recognized.
- Incentives should reflect the interdependence of the work.
7. Q: Why did the Soviet oil-drilling incentive reward the wrong activity?
A: Show answer
Workers received bonuses based on the number of feet drilled. Because many shallow holes were easier to produce than a few deep holes, employees optimized the rewarded measure rather than following geological advice to reach existing reserves. The result was substantial drilling activity but little useful oil discovery. The case demonstrates the difference between an easily counted output and the real objective. When a metric is only a proxy, leaders must test whether it remains aligned with value under the behavior it encourages. A more complete measure would have considered the quality and usefulness of discoveries, not simply drilling volume.
Study instruction:
- For a metric in your workplace, identify the true outcome it is meant to represent and test whether the measure can be achieved without that outcome.
Key point:
- A proxy measure can displace the real objective.
- Reward useful results and quality, not merely visible activity.
8. Q: What happened when an executive attached bonuses to employee improvement suggestions?
A: Show answer
The executive expected bonuses and prizes for validated ideas to increase innovation. Instead, employees became less inclined to develop suggestions. Before the program, some offered ideas because they wanted to improve working life; after money was attached, they focused on proposals with a potentially substantial payout and spent more effort negotiating compensation. The reward changed the perceived purpose of contributing from improving the organization to securing a personal award. This example shows that a reward can crowd out voluntary contribution and make people more selective or transactional. Leaders should check whether a new incentive damages an existing source of engagement.
Study instruction:
- Compare how employees might respond to an open improvement forum with and without a cash prize, and identify possible unintended effects.
Key point:
- A bonus can turn contribution into a bargaining exercise.
- Protect existing commitment before adding financial rewards.
9. Q: What is the overjustification effect described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The overjustification effect occurs when an external reward for an activity that a person already finds satisfying shifts the perceived reason for doing it. Instead of attributing the activity to interest, enjoyment, or personal value, the person may begin to see it as something performed to obtain the reward. When the reward disappears, participation can fall because the original intrinsic reason has been weakened or displaced. The chapter illustrates this with children who initially preferred a toy but later played with it less after receiving snacks for using it. The risk is greatest when an incentive is attached to behavior that already has strong intrinsic appeal.
Study instruction:
- Explain the overjustification effect using a hobby, learning activity, or voluntary workplace contribution that might be undermined by payment.
Key point:
- External rewards can change how people explain their own behavior.
- Removing a reward may not restore the original level of interest.
10. Q: Why can paying someone to do an activity they already enjoy reduce later participation?
A: Show answer
Payment may cause the person to interpret participation as a means of earning rather than as an expression of interest or enjoyment. Once that economic frame becomes dominant, stopping payment removes the salient reason for acting. The chapter's reading example describes a child who reads more while receiving money but reads less after the payments stop, even compared with the earlier period. This does not mean that all rewards destroy intrinsic motivation; the risk is a design consideration, especially for activities that were already self-motivating. Leaders should identify the existing reason for the behavior before deciding whether an external reward adds value.
Study instruction:
- Identify a behavior already performed willingly and decide whether it needs a reward or would benefit more from recognition, resources, or autonomy.
Key point:
- Do not assume every desired behavior needs a cash incentive.
- Preserve intrinsic satisfaction when it is already present.
11. Q: How can an incentive change the way people frame a behavior?
A: Show answer
A reward can signal what kind of issue the behavior is. A moral frame emphasizes responsibility and what is right; a social frame emphasizes relationships, expectations, and the opinions of others; an economic frame emphasizes price, gain, and loss. The chapter argues that adding a fee or bonus can convert a responsibility that people previously understood morally or socially into a financial calculation. That shift may make the behavior appear acceptable as long as the price is paid. Leaders should therefore consider the message carried by the incentive, not just its immediate effect on compliance.
Study instruction:
- Take a rule that is currently framed as a responsibility and write how a cash penalty might change the way people interpret it.
Key point:
- Incentives communicate a frame as well as a consequence.
- Economic framing can displace moral and social reasons for acting.
12. Q: Why did the daycare late-pickup fee increase lateness instead of reducing it?
A: Show answer
Parents were expected to collect their children at 3:30 p.m., but a small number arrived late. The daycare introduced a $5 charge for each 30 minutes of lateness. Instead of making punctuality more compelling, the fee gave parents a price for additional care. A behavior previously understood as keeping a promise and respecting staff became an economic choice: some parents treated the fee as an acceptable cost for extra time. The chapter reports that lateness became much more common. The lesson is not that every penalty fails, but that pricing a breach can unintentionally legitimize it.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a late fee, service charge, or compliance penalty and ask whether it deters the behavior or effectively sells permission to do it.
Key point:
- A penalty can turn a social obligation into a purchasable option.
- Test how the target group will interpret the price.
13. Q: What does the water-balloon incident illustrate about spontaneous influence?
A: Show answer
When a father collected his son after the boy had thrown water balloons at cars, the father's opening response could have framed the misconduct economically, socially, or morally. Threatening punishment would have emphasized external consequences; saying the son had embarrassed the family would have emphasized reputation. Instead, the father asked the boy to imagine how a driver would feel if a hard object struck the windshield. That response invited empathy and framed the behavior as harm to another person. The example shows that leaders and parents communicate a motivational frame through their first words, even before a formal reward or sanction is considered.
Study instruction:
- Prepare three possible opening statements for a behavior problem, one economic, one social, and one moral, then compare their likely effects.
Key point:
- The first response can establish the frame for the entire conversation.
- Moral perspective-taking can be more durable than a threat alone.
14. Q: What principle does the chapter draw from Jay Inslee's response to a question about penalties?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's account of a March 2020 press conference, then-governor Jay Inslee was asked about penalties for violating public-health restrictions. Rather than lead with fines or incarceration, he emphasized the risk of harming a family member and the principle of community responsibility. The authors use this example to show how a leader can resist turning a moral and social responsibility into a calculation about punishment. The example concerns the communication frame, not a claim that enforcement rules are unnecessary. Leaders can explain consequences while keeping the primary reason for compliance grounded in protecting others.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a compliance announcement so that it explains the human purpose of the rule before describing formal consequences.
Key point:
- Lead with the responsibility the rule protects.
- Do not let a penalty become the only reason people comply.
15. Q: How does Danny Meyer's pooled-tip approach illustrate aligned incentives?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes hospitality leader Danny Meyer as using pooled tips because exceptional customer experiences depend on the whole team. A shared pool treats gratuities as a reflection of collective service rather than a prize for one employee's individual effort. Meyer also emphasizes that employees serve because they enjoy the work and belong to a culture that values service, not merely to earn tips. The arrangement aligns the structural reward with cooperation while relying on personal and social motivation to sustain service quality. It illustrates how a modest incentive can support a team norm instead of creating competition for individual credit.
Study instruction:
- Examine a service team and decide whether its reward arrangement encourages helping colleagues or competing for visible individual results.
Key point:
- Shared outcomes may require shared rewards.
- Culture and enjoyment should not be replaced by tip-driven behavior.
16. Q: When does the chapter consider extrinsic incentives appropriate?
A: Show answer
The chapter does not reject rewards or sanctions. It gives examples where incentives support a broader influence strategy: peer-group repayment pressure at Grameen Bank, legal consequences in a campaign to protect workers from HIV risk, and a small recognition token for people who report unsafe water-filtering practices. These examples differ in form, but each connects a consequence to a vital behavior and operates alongside social or moral influence. The practical test is whether the incentive reinforces a clearly defined behavior without overwhelming other reasons to act. The chapter's recommendation is thoughtful alignment, not the elimination of external consequences.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed incentive, state the vital behavior, the other sources of influence supporting it, and the specific gap the incentive is meant to address.
Key point:
- Extrinsic incentives can be useful when properly aligned.
- They should reinforce a wider influence strategy rather than stand alone.
17. Q: What does the guideline 'Think Small' mean in incentive design?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends using rewards no larger than necessary. Small incentives can acknowledge progress, make a behavior visible, or provide an immediate consequence without turning the activity into a high-stakes financial contest. Their symbolic value may exceed their monetary value because they communicate recognition, status, belonging, or accomplishment. Large rewards can distract people from the purpose of the behavior, encourage gaming, or crowd out personal and social motivation. The appropriate size depends on the behavior and context, but the design should begin with the smallest effective reinforcement and be monitored for unintended effects.
Study instruction:
- Design a low-cost recognition option for a desired behavior and explain what symbolic meaning it should communicate.
Key point:
- A small reward can be powerful when its meaning is clear.
- Use the minimum incentive needed to support the behavior.
18. Q: What does the Johns Hopkins alcohol-moderation example demonstrate about small incentives?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a hospital study involving patients admitted for alcohol use under a moderation program. Staff linked daily privileges, including food, phone access, and visiting arrangements, to whether patients stayed within a target consumption level. The chapter reports that experimental subjects were 60 percent more likely to reach the target than control patients who were simply told how much to drink. The example illustrates how immediate, modest consequences can support a specific behavior in a structured setting. It should be understood as the chapter's reported case, not as a general clinical protocol or a substitute for professional treatment planning.
Study instruction:
- Identify the target behavior, the immediate consequence, and the comparison group in the example; distinguish the reported finding from a universal treatment recommendation.
Key point:
- Immediate consequences can support measurable behavior change.
- A case example does not establish a one-size-fits-all clinical intervention.
19. Q: How does the chapter describe voucher-based incentives in cocaine-recovery programs?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses a method developed by Stephen Higgins in which outpatients submitted urine samples three times a week. Participants who produced three negative samples received a bonus voucher exchangeable for goods and services supplied by the research staff. The chapter reports higher completion of a 12-week program among participants receiving incentives than among non-voucher subjects, and notes that the long-term effects were also encouraging. The authors explicitly caution that vouchers alone would not be sufficient for lasting change: all six sources of influence are needed. The lesson is that an immediate, modest reward can reinforce progress when embedded in a broader support system.
Study instruction:
- Map the contingency in the example: required evidence, qualifying behavior, timing of the reward, and the wider supports needed for sustained change.
Key point:
- A reward is linked to a clearly observed vital behavior.
- The chapter presents incentives as one component of a broader change system.
20. Q: Why does the frequent-flyer example show that incentives can influence behavior beyond their cash value?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that travelers may take unnecessary flights to retain status or route purchases through an associated credit card to accumulate points. The program's effect is not explained only by the monetary value of free travel. Status badges and elite categories also communicate achievement and social standing. This demonstrates that a reward can combine economic, personal, and social motivation: points provide a material benefit, progress can feel satisfying, and status can signal recognition. It also shows why organizations should anticipate behaviors that participants may adopt to maximize a reward, including actions that do not create genuine value.
Study instruction:
- For a loyalty program, list the economic benefit, status signal, and potentially wasteful behavior the design could encourage.
Key point:
- Incentive value includes symbolic and social components.
- Status programs can generate behavior beyond the intended purchase pattern.
21. Q: Why was the brass goose award effective despite its low monetary value?
A: Show answer
A consulting firm awarded a small brass goose to senior executives who completed assigned training behaviors and reported back to their trainer. The award cost little, yet executives completed lengthy readings, case studies, forms, and practice assignments to earn it. The chapter explains that the token's value came from its symbolic message and from the personal and social meaning attached to recognition, not its resale price. The case illustrates that a reward can make progress visible and create a shared ritual. It also reinforces the principle that an incentive need not be expensive when it supports meaningful behavior and group identity.
Study instruction:
- Design a symbolic award for a learning or improvement process and specify the behavior that earns it, rather than the outcome alone.
Key point:
- Recognition can make small achievements visible.
- Symbolic meaning may be more motivating than monetary value.
22. Q: How did The Other Side Academy use privileges and shirt colors as structural motivation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes students progressing through increasingly responsible work and privileges. They move from basic tasks to more complex jobs and receive different shirt colors as they advance, eventually reaching a senior level. Later privileges include a modest monthly allowance and permission to leave campus to use it. These markers make progress tangible and give students visible evidence of growing responsibility. The rewards are small in financial terms but significant in status and autonomy. The case shows how a structured progression can reinforce repeated accomplishments while giving participants a clear picture of the next achievable step.
Study instruction:
- Create a progression ladder for a training or rehabilitation program with observable milestones, earned privileges, and safeguards against arbitrary advancement.
Key point:
- Progressive privileges can make development tangible.
- Rewards can represent trust, status, and increased responsibility.
23. Q: What caution should be drawn from the group-home example involving suicide attempts and TV privileges?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that a group home for troubled teenage girls restricted television privileges after an attempted suicide and that attempts subsequently fell to zero. This is a description of the case as presented by the authors, not evidence that punitive restrictions are a safe or effective general response to self-harm. Suicidal behavior requires qualified clinical assessment, safeguarding, supportive care, and attention to underlying causes. A punitive response may suppress disclosure, increase shame, or create other risks if applied without specialist oversight. The responsible lesson for practitioners is to avoid copying a single reported contingency outside its context and never substitute discipline for appropriate mental-health care.
Study instruction:
- Discuss why a reported incentive result cannot automatically be transferred to a safeguarding or clinical setting; identify the specialist review required before any intervention.
Key point:
- Do not treat the example as a clinical protocol.
- Safety, disclosure, and professional care take priority over punitive incentives.
24. Q: How did the open-pit mine use small team rewards to improve safe driving?
A: Show answer
The mine used vehicle data on speed, acceleration, and braking to calculate daily risky-driving scores. Drivers were assigned to five-person teams, and each team's score was determined by its worst individual score. Teams with the best scores received modest prizes such as caps, shirts, and mugs. The chapter reports rapid improvement in safe driving after the system began. The design combined monitoring, peer accountability, team identity, and small recognition. It made unsafe behavior visible to the group and gave team members a reason to encourage one another rather than relying only on individual punishment.
Study instruction:
- Sketch a team-based safety scorecard and assess whether the weakest-member rule encourages coaching or could instead create blame and concealment.
Key point:
- Small prizes can reinforce a team safety norm.
- Peer accountability must be designed to encourage support, not scapegoating.
25. Q: Why should leaders reward behavior rather than only final results?
A: Show answer
Results may take time to appear, may be influenced by factors outside an individual's control, or may conceal how they were achieved. The chapter recommends recognizing the vital behaviors that are expected to produce sustainable results, including early improvements and sincere attempts to apply a new method. If leaders reward only final outcomes, employees may conclude that process quality, collaboration, and ethical conduct do not matter. Rewarding the right behavior helps establish habits before the final result becomes visible. It also reduces the risk of celebrating a successful outcome achieved through shortcuts or conduct that undermines long-term performance.
Study instruction:
- For one performance goal, define three observable behaviors that should be recognized before the final outcome is achieved.
Key point:
- The behavior rewarded is the behavior people learn to repeat.
- Outcome-only rewards can hide harmful methods or short-term luck.
26. Q: Why are incremental improvements important to recognize?
A: Show answer
The chapter observes that organizations often reserve praise for exceptional achievements even though people benefit from recognition for smaller improvements. Early progress matters because new behaviors may need to be repeated for some time before major results become visible. Recognizing a sincere attempt to use the vital behavior can reinforce learning, persistence, and confidence. Recognition should be specific: it should identify what the person did and why that action supports the goal. Generic praise is less useful than feedback that makes the desired behavior understandable and repeatable.
Study instruction:
- Choose a developing skill and write specific recognition for an observable improvement, explaining how it contributes to the larger objective.
Key point:
- Small improvements are opportunities to reinforce the desired process.
- Specific recognition teaches people which actions to repeat.
27. Q: What does the tea-service example from Kaizen illustrate about rewarding effort and process?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts a group of servers who noticed predictable patterns in tea consumption and calculated how much tea each table actually needed. Their adjustment reduced tea-leaf use by half, but the financial saving was modest. The group nevertheless received the company's presidential gold medal because the judges valued the way the team identified and implemented a useful improvement. Other suggestions produced larger savings, but the award recognized the process the organization wanted repeated. The example illustrates that leaders can signal priorities by honoring disciplined problem-solving, observation, and practical improvement rather than ranking contributions solely by immediate financial return.
Study instruction:
- Describe an improvement whose process quality is high even if its immediate savings are small; propose a fair way to recognize it.
Key point:
- Rewarding the improvement process can build repeatable capability.
- The largest short-term saving is not always the most valuable behavior to reinforce.
28. Q: How can performance rewards undermine teamwork when leaders praise individual results?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses sports coaching to show the gap between stated values and rewarded behavior. A coach may speak about teamwork but celebrate only the player who scores. Teammates then learn that individual visibility matters more than assists, coordination, or shared success. The same pattern appears in workplaces when employees are told to collaborate but rewarded only for personal sales, output, or billable hours. People pay close attention to what leaders recognize, not only what leaders say. To strengthen teamwork, the reward criteria must make cooperative behaviors visible and valued.
Study instruction:
- Compare a team's stated values with its award criteria and identify which behaviors the current system actually teaches.
Key point:
- Recognition is a practical signal of organizational priorities.
- Teamwork requires rewards that acknowledge contributions to shared performance.
29. Q: What does the family-enabling example reveal about unintended reinforcement?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a family whose words condemn a loved one's drug use while its actions provide free rent, access to cars, and bail whenever problems occur. Those benefits may unintentionally make the harmful behavior easier to continue. The mismatch between verbal disapproval and material support sends a stronger practical signal than the family's stated message. The example illustrates that structural motivation is not limited to formal compensation systems; everyday privileges and assistance can reinforce behavior. A thoughtful response should distinguish support for the person from support that removes consequences or enables continued harm, and should seek appropriate professional guidance.
Study instruction:
- Map the benefits and consequences surrounding a recurring harmful behavior; distinguish compassionate support from reinforcement of the behavior itself.
Key point:
- Actions can reward behavior even when words condemn it.
- Review informal benefits as well as formal incentive policies.
30. Q: How does the chapter use savings and tax policy to show that incentives shape behavior?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the idea that national differences in saving behavior must reflect fixed differences in character. It notes that tax treatment can alter the relative attractiveness of saving and borrowing: interest on savings was taxable in the United States while consumer-debt interest had been deductible, whereas the chapter describes a different historical treatment in Japan. The point is that behavior often responds to the surrounding economic structure. Before attributing a pattern to motivation or culture, leaders should examine whether policy makes one choice materially easier or more rewarding than another. The example is historical and should not be treated as a statement of current tax law.
Study instruction:
- Select a behavior often explained as a matter of character and list the policy or financial arrangements that may influence it.
Key point:
- Structural conditions can shape behavior across populations.
- Check the date and jurisdiction before relying on historical tax examples.
31. Q: What does the Road Warrior award reveal about misaligned rewards and employee retention?
A: Show answer
A consulting firm awarded a cash prize to the employee who spent the most days on the road. The award was intended to recognize valuable consulting work, but the company wanted talented consultants to remain and develop their careers. The chapter reports that for four consecutive years the winners left the firm in the same year, citing work-life balance concerns. The incentive celebrated extensive travel, even though excessive travel contributed to the condition driving people away. Leaders should therefore test rewards against broader strategic outcomes such as retention, well-being, and sustainable performance, not just the metric that is easiest to count.
Study instruction:
